# Revision 7 Research Questions (Wave 3)

Created: 2026-07-12. This is the open research file for Revision 7. It follows the Wave 2 formula (`../done_researching_second_wave/research_questions_revision6.md`) with the improvements noted below. The two answered waves — `../done_researching/` (first wave, seven files) and `../done_researching_second_wave/` (26 items, all answered 2026-07-09) — are the record of everything already resolved; do not re-research what they settle.

**Status: OPEN.** Wave 3 is content-driven: it feeds the four new Revision 7 chapters (The Wall System, Masonry Craft, Solar/Battery/Energy Independence, Personal Project Finance) and the double-wythe cavity-wall path added as the owner's alternative. The Research Pause Rule applies: no new-chapter section is written against an open item here.

> **Start here (2026-07-12): read `RESEARCH_EXPANSION_PLAYBOOK.md` first.** Your first job is to BUILD the question bank to 100+ deep questions, not to answer them. Five background agents wrote draft banks to `drafts/` (alternative materials, block-making tools, mortar, and problem-and-solution sets) — review, curate, and merge them in, then continue chapter by chapter per the playbook. The heaviest expansion is the non-code material: creative ideas, tools, ingredients, methods, options in detail, and real-world problems-and-solutions worldwide. **Chapter numbers shifted in the 26-chapter reorg:** the seeds below still use old numbers — Masonry Craft is now ch10, Solar is ch16, Personal Finance is ch24, and the new ch9 is Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes (a major new research target). Re-tag `Lands in:` to the 26-chapter order in `../STRUCTURE_PLAN.md` as you merge.

## Instructions For The Research AI

1. Work one numbered item at a time. Append the answer directly below the item, starting with a bold **Answered (date):** line. Do not delete or reword the questions.
2. **Check the local library first.** `../../revision6_code_library/` (project root) holds downloaded official documents; its README lists the tested online routes (UpCodes for full Oregon code text, etc.). Where an item names a likely file, read it before searching the web.
3. Prefer official sources in this order: adopted code text, Oregon statute or administrative rule, agency guidance (BCD, DEQ, OHA, OWRD, CCB, OPUC, Oregon OSHA), federal source (EPA, OSHA, FTC, IRS, Copyright Office), then standards-body pages (TMS, ASTM, NCMA/CMHA, BIA, ACI, IAPMO, UL, NEC), then professional secondary sources. Give the URL for every source used, inline with the claim it supports.
4. Quote the controlling value exactly: section number, limit, date, edition. Name the edition whenever the answer depends on it, and record the date you checked. **Edition-currency trigger:** the 2026 ORSC (2024 IRC base), 2026 OPSC (2024 UPC base), and next OMSC are anticipated to adopt near October 1, 2026; tag anything edition-dependent and note the recheck.
5. If the answer depends on county, city, product, utility, lender, or timing, do not flatten it. State the dependency and what the builder must confirm, and with whom.
6. Add a **Lands in:** line to each item naming the target chapter/section (Wave 3 exists to feed specific new content). Where research shows the item belongs elsewhere, say so.
7. End each answer with a status tag on its own line, from this fixed list:
   - `SETTLED` — answered with an authoritative source; the manuscript may state it as fact.
   - `SETTLED-WITH-EDITION` — answered, but the value resets when the named future edition adopts; the manuscript notes the edition.
   - `BLOCKED-PAYWALL` — the controlling text is a purchased standard (TMS 402/602, ASTM, BIA/NCMA proprietary); record what free sources give and what the purchase would add.
   - `NOT-RESEARCHABLE-AHJ` — a permit-time or jurisdiction fact; state exactly what to ask and whom.
   - `NOT-RESEARCHABLE-PRO` — an attorney, CPA, insurance, engineer, or lender question; state exactly what to bring to that professional.
   - `OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE` — a design decision that is the owner's to make, not a researchable fact; state the options and the trade-offs so the owner can choose (the double-wythe path surfaces several of these).
8. When every item in a section is tagged, add a **Section summary** listing the items by tag.
9. Do not write manuscript text. Any anchor sentence drafted for the writer must obey the Complete Catalog Of Writing Problems in `../PROBLEMS_AND_GOALS.md`. New questions found during research go to the end under "New Questions Found During Research," same format.
10. **Sub-agent throttle:** if this wave is run with background agents, use at most three at once, one or two preferred. Wave 2's 14-parallel-agent burst is not repeated; it exhausted the token allowance.

Per-item answer files are preserved in `answers/` (item_NN.md), mirroring Wave 2.

---


## Consolidated Per-Chapter Question Bank (Wave 3, merged 2026-07-12)

The candidate questions from the 21 pre-research draft banks (in `drafts/`), organized by chapter in the 36-chapter order. Questions sit under `## <Chapter Topic Name>` headings, numbered within each chapter. A question serving two chapters may appear under each. Answering happens in R7-5: append the answer under each item, then its status tag. The how-to-use chapter and some well-covered existing chapters carry few or no new questions.

File: `_MERGED_1_partI_and_design.md`

Contains: Purpose, Principles, and How to Use This Book; Oregon Code and Compliance Map; Land, Site, and Feasibility; Legal, Permits, and Owner-Builder Rules; Soil, Ground Prep, Drainage, and Foundation Risk; Structural System and Concrete Design; What Concrete Uniquely Enables: Designing With Mass; Designing for Centuries: Durability Engineering; Passive and Bioclimatic Design.

## Purpose, Principles, and How to Use This Book

No candidate questions were found for this chapter across the 21 draft banks. The pre-research agents were assigned technical and systems topics (materials, tools, mortar, problem-and-solution catalogs, resilience, mass design, durability, aesthetics, water, site development, body and mind, land, build sequence, documentation, roof, code and permits, interiors, tools, business); none targeted the introductory how-to-use-this-book chapter. The seed file (`research_questions_revision7.md`, Sections A-D) also has no items landing here.

## Oregon Code and Compliance Map

1. Does ORSC R104.11 as adopted in Oregon, distinct from the base IRC text, require the building official to give a written response with stated reasons when an alternative-method request is denied, and what does that written denial need to contain to be useful for an appeal? [CODE] (controlling agency: Oregon Building Codes Division and the local building official)
   Leads: the base IRC R104.11 text outside Oregon reportedly requires a written response with reasons; Oregon's own adopted text needs a direct check.

2. Does R104.4's acceptance of written, certified reports of inspection by approved agencies or individuals extend to an out-of-state evaluation report, such as an ICC-ES report, an IAPMO UES report, or another state's earthen-building-code compliance finding, as evidence toward an Oregon R104.11 approval, or does Oregon require independent, in-state testing regardless of any outside report? [CODE] (controlling agency: Oregon Building Codes Division and the local building official)

3. Where can the actual list of past and current Statewide Alternate Methods be browsed, and what does one full example ruling look like from start to finish: its format, its level of technical detail, and any conditions attached? [CODE] (controlling agency: Oregon Building Codes Division)
   Leads: "Statewide Alternate Method No. 26-01" (continued reference to the 2022 OSSC for nonprescriptive design) is a confirmed example name; the full public list was not retrieved in this pass.

4. Does a granted Alternate Method Ruling apply automatically and immediately in every Oregon jurisdiction, or does a local building official retain any discretion to decline to apply a statewide ruling to a specific project? [CODE] (controlling agency: Oregon Building Codes Division; enforcement duty on local jurisdictions under OAR 918-008-0110)

5. What is the complete, current county-by-county list of which Oregon building departments are run locally, which are administered directly by the state Building Codes Division, and which are contracted to a private firm, for the specific counties realistic for this project's site search? [CODE] (controlling agency: Oregon Building Codes Division, Local Building Department Directory)

6. For a county where BCD directly administers the building program with no local department, does that change the alternative-materials process at all: does a BCD field inspector hold the same R104.11 approval authority as a local building official, or does every alternative-materials request in a BCD-direct county route through Salem? [CODE] (controlling agency: Oregon Building Codes Division)

7. Under what conditions, which wind or seismic design values, which building height, would a residential project in western Oregon's seismic design categories cross into an OSSC-governed special-inspection trigger rather than staying under the ORSC, and is that a realistic risk for a two-story single-family concrete or masonry home at this project's scale? [CODE] (controlling agency: Oregon Building Codes Division; ORSC versus OSSC applicability)

8. Do ACI 318 or ACI 332-20, already cited in the existing code-map chapter as the engineered-design basis for above-grade concrete and masonry walls in Oregon's higher seismic design categories, themselves require a form of special inspection or a testing frequency as part of using that design basis, independent of whether the ORSC separately mandates it? [CODE] (controlling agency: the engineer of record, applying ACI 318 or ACI 332-20 as the referenced standard)

9. Is Oregon's own adopted Appendix AS, Strawbale Construction, in the 2023 ORSC adopted by ordinance in any of the specific rural counties realistic for this project, given that the appendix is confirmed optional, not mandatory unless specifically referenced in the adopting ordinance? [CODE] (controlling agency: the individual county's adopting ordinance; confirm at permit time) (permit-time item)

10. What was the actual multi-year advocacy and drafting process that got Appendix S and AS, Strawbale Construction, adopted first as an ICC model-code appendix and then into the Oregon-specific code cycle, and does that history point to a realistic path, through a body such as the Cob Research Institute or the Development Center for Appropriate Technology, for a future compressed-earth-block or rammed-earth appendix, on what kind of timeline measured in years rather than months? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
   Leads: named advocates found: Martin Hammer, David Eisenberg of the Development Center for Appropriate Technology, John Fordice of the Cob Research Institute; the Northwest Eco Building Guild's Code Innovations Database is a named resource cataloging officially approved sustainable-building solutions nationwide.

11. Beyond Northwest Code Professionals, which other private firms currently hold building-department contracts with rural or small Oregon jurisdictions, such as SAFEbuilt or others, and what is the complete list of Oregon jurisdictions each one serves? [CODE] (controlling agency: individual contracting jurisdictions; verify the current client list directly with each firm)

12. Does a contracted building-department firm's inspector or plan reviewer carry the same Oregon-certification requirements and the same legal authority, such as stop-work orders, correction notices, and final sign-off, as a directly employed county building official, or is there any functional difference in what a private-firm reviewer can and cannot decide? [CODE] (controlling agency: Oregon Building Codes Division certification standards apply regardless of employer)

## Land, Site, and Feasibility

1. Septic type selection on a hard site (Oregon DEQ terms). For high water table / shallow or slow-perc soil, walk the ladder: conventional gravity vs pressure-distribution vs sand filter vs ATU vs mound/capping fill, with siting, required reserve/replacement area, and the cost climb. What does the site/perc evaluation decide? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [COST] [CODE]
   Leads: Oregon DEQ onsite/subsurface wastewater program, soil and perc evaluation gates the whole feasibility question; reserve area is mandatory.

2. Well siting and yield, the independence question at the source. Interpreting a drawdown/yield test, OWRD exempt-groundwater limits, required separations from septic/property lines/contamination sources, and the low-yield-well fix (large storage cistern + booster pump buffering a trickle well). [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
   Leads: OWRD exempt-use groundwater framework; low-yield homesteads commonly solve gpm shortfalls with a cistern buffer feeding a pressure pump.

3. The driveway and site access that quietly break the budget. Grade, culvert, and drainage; base-rock depth to carry concrete and pump trucks; fire-apparatus turnaround/access; and why the durable driveway usually gets built first. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [COST] [METHOD]

4. Water independence as layered redundancy, well + rainwater + storage. Legality of potable roof-rainwater in Oregon, cistern sizing for dry-season autonomy, the first-flush + multi-stage filtration + UV train, low-pH corrosion control, and cross-connection/backflow rules when mixing well and rain sources. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE]
   Leads: permies / AZ1863 / NTO Tank, first-flush + cartridge stages + UV for potable; low-pH rain corrodes copper (neutralize); design sources so they can't cross-connect; Oregon permits roof-collected rainwater.

5. Does ORS 455.446/455.447's "essential facility" and "special occupancy structure" definition ever reach a single-family owner-built dwelling, under any circumstance (home occupation, accessory structure use, future use change), or is a single-family home categorically outside the statute's restricted-construction list? [CODE]

6. For any coastal or estuarine Oregon parcel this project might consider, what does the DOGAMI regulatory tsunami inundation map show for depth and modeled arrival time at that specific location, and how does that compare to the evacuation time available to reach mapped high ground on foot? [CODE] [METHOD]

7. For a property with a single-access rural driveway crossing a culvert or seasonal watercourse, what culvert sizing, headwall/outlet protection, and inspection routine prevents the washout failure mode described in Part 1, and what does a second/alternate emergency-access path (a graded but unpaved secondary route, a neighbor easement, a helicopter-accessible clearing) cost to establish at design time versus after a road-out event? [METHOD] [COST] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

8. What is the actual ASTM C216-style weathering index (freezing-cycle days multiplied by average annual winter rainfall) for the specific build site's location, calculated from NOAA or Oregon State University Extension climate data, rather than a general Willamette-Valley-versus-Cascades assumption? [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

9. What is the actual NOAA Climate Normals monthly precipitation profile for the station nearest this project's parcel, and what is the resulting length in days of the driest expected stretch to size storage against? [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
   Leads: NOAA NCEI U.S. Climate Normals Quick Access tool, unverified for the specific station.

10. How does a precast concrete cistern's delivery and crane-set requirement interact with this project's site access and staging constraints already flagged in the land-and-feasibility chapter, and at what tank size does precast stop being a realistic option for this specific parcel? [COST] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

11. What is the actual dollar and drainfield-size difference between a septic system designed for full wastewater flow and one designed with graywater diversion credited against it, for this project's household size, and does Oregon's onsite-wastewater sizing rule give a graywater system any formal design-flow credit, or does the drainfield still have to be sized as if all flow reaches it? [CODE] [COST]

12. For a marginal septic site (the kind the land-and-feasibility chapter already treats as a purchase risk), does a combination of graywater diversion plus a composting or urine-diverting toilet reduce the required drainfield size enough to change a site from "fails standard evaluation" to "passes with a smaller or simpler system," and is that combination something a septic site evaluator will actually credit at evaluation time or only after the fact? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [CODE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

13. Reading ORS 537.545 directly (not a secondary summary), what is the exact, current, fully reconciled text of every exempt groundwater use category and its cap, including how HB 3372's 2026 changes are now codified into the section, since two secondary sources gave inconsistent detail on the cannabis/hemp treatment? [CODE]
   Leads: ORS 537.545 primary statute text; HB 3372 (2025) enrolled bill text, both flagged for direct verification rather than reliance on secondary summaries.

14. Does this project's specific parcel sit inside, adjacent to, or outside any designated critical groundwater area or groundwater mitigation program area (the several Umatilla Basin areas, Cow Valley, the Dalles, Cooper-Bull Mountain, the newly designated Harney Basin, or the Deschutes mitigation program area), and if so, what does that designation actually change about the exempt domestic-well path this project assumes? [CODE]
   Leads: OWRD Groundwater Administrative Areas / Critical Groundwater Areas map and rule set, unverified for this specific parcel.

15. Beyond the 15,000-gallon-a-day domestic exemption, does this project's plan for any outbuilding, workshop, home-based business, or future ADU (already flagged as a feasibility item in the land-and-feasibility chapter) push combined water use into the industrial/commercial exempt category's separate 5,000-gallon-a-day cap, and does that change how the well or the water system should be sized and permitted from the start? [CODE] [METHOD]

16. What exactly does OWRD require to record and register an exempt groundwater use (the $300 fee and well-constructor map filing already found in this pass), and is that a step the owner has to trigger directly, or does the well driller handle it as part of the standard well-completion paperwork? [CODE] [COST]

17. Is there any meaningful legal difference in Oregon between water rights for a drilled well versus a developed spring versus rooftop rainwater catchment versus a pond, given all four might realistically be considered on a rural parcel, and does combining more than one of them on the same property trigger any additional water-right filing beyond what each source needs on its own? [CODE]

18. Does Oregon's rooftop-rainwater exemption under ORS 537.141 carry any structure-purpose limitation similar to the one reported for Washington State (limiting the exemption to catchment from a structure with some other primary purpose), and would a purpose-built, non-habitable catchment roof (a large carport-style collection structure with no other function) fall outside that exemption if such a limitation exists? [CODE]

19. Where an OWRD-designated critical groundwater area or a mitigation program applies, what actual permitting or mitigation path exists for a household that still wants a domestic well (paying into a mitigation bank, buying and retiring another water right, or another mechanism), and what does that cost in the areas most likely to affect a rural Oregon parcel search? [CODE] [COST]

20. What does a documented case of a neighboring well going dry or declining in yield over time (from drought, a new nearby high-capacity well, or aquifer decline) look like in practice for a rural Oregon groundwater basin, and does this project's site-selection process need to pull multi-year yield trend data for nearby OWRD-logged wells rather than a single point-in-time yield figure? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

21. If a candidate parcel has a spring or hillside seep, what does a full spring-development project (cutoff wall, collection bed, spring box, overflow, service line) cost and take to build compared to drilling a well of equivalent reliable yield, and what Oregon-specific water-rights, construction-standard, and water-quality-testing rules apply to a developed spring versus a drilled well? [CODE] [COST] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

22. Does a gravity-fed spring source (no pump required if the spring sits above the house) meaningfully strengthen this project's power-outage resilience compared to any well-based source, which always needs a pump, and is that resilience benefit worth actively favoring a spring-capable parcel during site selection? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

23. Is a small pond or open reservoir (fed by a seasonal stream, a spring, or graded surface runoff) a realistic supplementary non-potable source for irrigation, livestock, or fire-suppression reserve on a rural Oregon parcel, and what water-rights and dam-safety thresholds (size and holding-capacity triggers) turn a farm pond into a regulated structure requiring its own permit? [CODE] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

24. Does a multi-year drought pattern (several consecutive below-average wet seasons, not just one dry summer) realistically threaten this project's design if the cistern is sized only against a single average dry season, and what regional drought-frequency data for the specific basin should inform a margin above the single-season minimum? [TEST-TO-PROVE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

25. What emergency water-source-sharing or mutual-aid practice exists among rural Oregon neighbors during a genuine local water crisis (a dry well, a contaminated source), and does the site-selection process (already weighing road access and distance to services) have any bearing on whether this kind of informal resilience network is realistic at a candidate parcel? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

26. What is the actual ORSC section and current text for the non-habitable detached accessory-structure permit exemption (200 sq ft general, 400 sq ft on a 2-plus-acre parcel with a 20-foot setback), and does it change under the anticipated 2026 ORSC adoption? [CODE]
   Leads: general-source figures found (200 sq ft / 15 ft height; 400 sq ft on 2+ acres with 20 ft setback); exact current ORSC section number and the 2026-edition status are unverified.

27. How should the site plan resolve conflicts when the septic reserve area, the well setback, the solar array's shade-free envelope, and a desired outbuilding location overlap, and what is the right order to lock each of those onto the site plan first? [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

28. What is the exact current ORSC/OSSC section and text for the Oregon 4-foot retaining-wall engineering and permit threshold, including the surcharge trigger that applies even under 4 feet, and how does "surcharge" get defined for a wall that supports a driveway, a block-yard pad, or a building pad rather than plain soil? [CODE]
   Leads: general and Oregon-specific sources converge on a 4-foot footing-to-top threshold plus a surcharge trigger; exact ORSC/OSSC section number and current text unverified.

29. What is the right order to lock the major site-plan elements (house footprint, septic primary and reserve, well and its setback, solar array's shade-free zone, driveway and turnaround, shop, block-yard, and every outbuilding) onto one master site diagram, and which of those elements is genuinely fixed first versus flexible around the others? [METHOD] [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE]

30. What real setback distances (from the septic system, from the well, from the property line, from the house) govern where a generator shed, a battery shed, a block-yard, or a barn can legally sit, distinct from the zoning setbacks already covered in the Land, Site, and Feasibility chapter for the house itself? [CODE]

31. How does a future accessory dwelling unit (ADU), already flagged as a possible future-flexibility item in the Land, Site, and Feasibility chapter, interact with the placement of the shop, the block-yard, and the other outbuildings, if the owner wants to keep an ADU genuinely possible without knowing its exact future footprint yet? [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE] [METHOD]

32. Which Oregon-specific or regional land brokerages (beyond the national aggregators) have the strongest track record in the specific counties this project is likely to target, and what do they charge or how are they compensated? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [TOOLS]
   Leads: Oregon Farm and Home Brokers, Whitney Land Company, Mossy Oak Properties (unverified as to specific county coverage and current fee structure).

33. What does an Accredited Land Consultant (ALC) actually cost the buyer (commission structure, whether buyer-side representation is free to the buyer as in ordinary residential purchases), and is the fee worth it for a single owner-builder purchase versus a self-directed search? [COST] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]
   Leads: REALTORS Land Institute (rliland.com) find-a-consultant directory.

34. How does a buyer legally and effectively pull county assessor mailing-address data to contact absentee or inherited-land owners directly, and does Oregon or any target county restrict bulk use of assessor data for this purpose? [METHOD] [TOOLS]

35. What is the real success rate and cost per acquired parcel for a direct-mail or driving-for-dollars campaign aimed specifically at vacant rural land, as opposed to distressed houses (the use case most tools and guides are built for)? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [COST] [METHOD]

36. Which specific Oregon counties currently have active timber-company parcel-disposal programs or edge-parcel sales (as opposed to only bulk institutional transactions), and how does an individual buyer get on that list? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

37. What is the actual current listing volume and update frequency on the USDA FSA resale/inventory site for Oregon, and has any owner-builder successfully purchased a parcel through it recently? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [TOOLS]

38. Has BLM disposed of any parcels in Oregon in the last five years under its lands-and-realty sales program, and if so, in which counties and at what process and price? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

39. What specific Oregon rural real-estate Facebook groups, forums, or regional classifieds (outside the major national platforms) reliably carry off-market or pre-MLS listings, and how active are they currently? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [TOOLS]

40. What is the exact current tax-foreclosure sale calendar and procedure for the specific counties this project is likely to target, and how far in advance are the property lists published? [METHOD]

41. On a tax-foreclosure "as is, where is" quitclaim purchase, what specific pre-bid due diligence is actually possible (title history research without a preliminary title report, septic/well record lookup, zoning confirmation) within the typical notice window? [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

42. Has any owner-builder successfully financed construction on land purchased via county tax foreclosure or sheriff sale, given the quitclaim-deed, no-title-warranty condition, and if so, how was a lender or title company satisfied? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

43. What redemption-period rules, if any, apply to sheriff sales on vacant land in Oregon specifically, as opposed to occupied residential foreclosures? [CODE]

44. What specific liens, easements, or use restrictions have actually surfaced after the fact on Oregon tax-foreclosure land purchases, according to real buyer accounts, and how were they resolved or absorbed? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

45. How does a buyer distinguish, using county GIS and road department records, between a public road that is built and maintained, a public road that is platted but never built (a paper road), and a private road, before relying on frontage shown on a map? [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

46. What is the realistic cost and timeline to establish a recorded access easement across a neighboring parcel where none currently exists, from initial negotiation through recording, in a rural Oregon county? [COST] [METHOD]

47. What Oregon case law, beyond the general disfavored-and-difficult-to-establish standard, defines exactly what a buyer must prove to win an easement-by-necessity claim, and how long does that litigation typically take? [CODE] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

48. For a private road maintained by a road-maintenance association or informal neighbor agreement, what specific contract terms (cost-sharing formula, snow-plowing responsibility, dispute resolution, right to compel maintenance) should a buyer require before closing, beyond simply confirming the agreement exists? [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

49. What is the Oregon Fire Code's minimum unobstructed driving-surface width, vertical clearance, and turnaround/turnout requirement for a driveway serving a single dwelling, and at what point does a candidate parcel's existing driveway or access easement fail those minimums badly enough to require costly regrading or widening? [CODE]
   Leads: Oregon Fire Code Appendix D; 20 ft unobstructed width generally, 12 ft driving surface allowed serving two or fewer dwellings, 13'6" vertical clearance, turnaround required for dead ends over 150 ft, turnouts every 400 ft on roads over 400 ft, 26 ft width required on dead ends over 500 ft (all unverified, needs edition-specific confirmation).

50. How does a buyer check, before making an offer, whether a candidate parcel sits inside one of Oregon's designated critical or limited groundwater areas, and what specific restriction applies if it does? [METHOD] [CODE]
   Leads: OWRD groundwater administrative areas page; cross-reference Water Independence chapter for the full water-rights treatment.

51. What does OWRD's well-log database actually show a buyer about neighboring well density, depth, and reported yield, and how is that information used to screen for a shared or stressed aquifer before committing to a well-dependent parcel? [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

52. Are there specific Oregon basins or sub-areas, beyond the Deschutes and southern-Oregon (Midland/Rogue) cases already documented, with a recent history of domestic wells going dry from nearby heavy withdrawal, that a buyer should treat as an elevated-risk zone? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

53. What is the fastest and cheapest way for a buyer, during a short inspection period, to get a preliminary (not final permit-grade) read on septic feasibility, short of the full county site evaluation, to decide whether the full evaluation is worth ordering? [METHOD] [COST]
   Leads: county soil survey (NRCS Web Soil Survey) as a free first screen against known limiting soils; cross-reference the septic system-selection ladder already covered in the problems-and-solutions research (item E14).

54. Do county GIS portals in the likely target counties publish a soils layer or septic-suitability overlay that lets a buyer pre-screen a parcel online before paying for a site evaluation? [TOOLS] [METHOD]

55. For a parcel with a specific written grid-extension quote, what is the actual side-by-side lifecycle cost comparison against a solar-plus-battery system sized to the same house load, using this project's real appliance and heating loads rather than a generic house? [COST] [METHOD]

56. Do Oregon's investor-owned utilities (PGE, Pacific Power) and the various rural electric cooperatives differ meaningfully in their line-extension allowance and cost-recovery formulas, in a way that would favor targeting a parcel served by one over the other? [COST] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

57. What specific tools (PVWatts, a solar pathfinder, a phone app using the camera and compass) give a reliable early solar-access reading for a candidate building site before committing to a full site survey? [TOOLS] [METHOD]

58. How many hours of unobstructed winter-sun access on the south-facing wall and roof plane should a candidate site guarantee, at Oregon's latitude, to make both passive solar gain and a solar-electric array genuinely effective, and how is that measured on a site visit (sun-angle chart, shadow study, tool-based reading)? [METHOD]
   Leads: general passive-solar guidance citing 5-6 hours of direct winter sun on the south wall as a minimum (unverified for Oregon's specific latitude and cloud-cover pattern).

59. How is DOGAMI's SLIDO landslide database actually read and interpreted by a non-specialist buyer at the parcel level (what the different hazard/susceptibility layers mean, what a "yes" result requires as a follow-up), and what does a geotechnical landslide-risk consultation cost if SLIDO flags a concern? [TOOLS] [METHOD] [COST]

1. ## Soil, Ground Prep, Drainage, and Foundation Risk

1. What goes wrong when an owner-builder backfills against a foundation/stem wall too early or with the wrong material, and what backfill-and-compaction sequence prevents wall cracking and inward rotation? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
   Leads: heavy equipment compacting against an unbraced wall cracks it; backfill only after the slab/floor braces the top; free-draining granular backfill vs native clay against the wall.

2. On a sloping rural Oregon site with clay soils and a seasonal high water table, which perimeter and sub-slab drainage designs (footing drains, curtain drains, daylight outfall vs sump) actually keep the structure dry, and where do they clog or fail? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
   Leads: footing drain must sit at/below the footing wrapped in filter fabric + clean gravel; silt clogging of pipe/fabric; daylighted outfall preferred over sump where grade allows.

3. How do experienced builders handle expansive/high-plasticity clay and soft/organic soils, what site investigation, over-excavation, engineered structural fill, or pier options prevent differential settlement? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [COST]
   Leads: geotech survey treated as non-negotiable; compacted structural fill in controlled lifts; drilled piers/shafts on expansive clay; clay swells 10-15% wet; trees within 15-20 ft cause localized shrinkage/settlement.

4. Which surface-water and grading mistakes (negative slope to the house, downspouts dumping at the foundation, missing swales) cause the majority of chronic foundation moisture, and what grading/gutter details prevent them? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
   Leads: constant soil moisture matters most on expansive soils; positive slope away, extended downspout discharge, functional gutters.

5. What creative site-water strategies can an owner-builder combine on a rural lot with no municipal storm system (French drains, dry wells, rain gardens, swales, capillary breaks/mud-mats under footings), and how are they sized? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]

6. How do builders protect an open excavation through Oregon's wet season, sidewall collapse, softened bearing soil, frost in the trench, and keep a subgrade from turning to mud before the pour? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
   Leads: never pour on frozen or softened subgrade; lean-concrete mud-mat; benching/sloping or shoring for stability.

7. How do builders decide slab vs crawlspace vs basement on a wet Oregon site, and what practices manage a high water table / hydrostatic pressure (under-slab drainage, sump, head-rated waterproofing, flotation/uplift)? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
   Leads: waterproofing must be rated for the actual head; drainage to relieve pressure vs sealing against it; empty tanks/slabs can float.

8. For a property with a single-access rural driveway crossing a culvert or seasonal watercourse, what culvert sizing, headwall/outlet protection, and inspection routine prevents the washout failure mode described in Part 1, and what does a second/alternate emergency-access path (a graded but unpaved secondary route, a neighbor easement, a helicopter-accessible clearing) cost to establish at design time versus after a road-out event? [METHOD] [COST] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

9. Where does the diverted first-flush water go (a French drain, a rain garden, the ordinary site-drainage swale system already specified in the soil chapter), and does its contaminant load (roof grit, bird droppings, atmospheric deposition) require any particular disposal siting relative to the foundation, the septic drainfield, or a garden bed? [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

10. If a cistern is integrated into the foundation footprint (a dedicated basement room or a below-slab vault used purely for water storage), what does that do to the foundation's structural design, waterproofing detailing, and the foundation drainage hierarchy already specified in the soil chapter, and does it change the excavation and formwork sequence in the build-sequence chapter? [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

11. How does the block-yard's stormwater runoff get integrated into the whole-site drainage and erosion-control plan, given that a flat, compacted, sloped production pad is a new impervious-adjacent surface the original site drainage design did not account for? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

12. For this project's actual cut-and-fill needs (creating a level building pad, a driveway grade, or a block-yard pad on a sloped parcel), what wall type (gravity SRW, geogrid-reinforced SRW, gabion, owner-made CMU, cast concrete) fits each specific retained height and load, and where does the 4-foot Oregon threshold force a specific choice toward or away from owner-built options? [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE] [METHOD]

13. What terracing strategy (a series of shorter walls under the 4-foot unengineered threshold instead of one tall engineered wall) works for this project's specific slope, and what does terracing cost and gain in usable flat area compared with one tall wall? [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE] [METHOD] [COST]

## Structural System and Concrete Design

1. What causes honeycombing, voids, sand streaking, and cold joints in structural concrete walls/columns, and what consolidation (vibration), lift-height, and pour-rate practices prevent them? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
   Leads: over- vs under-vibration; cold joint when the delay between lifts exceeds initial set; form leakage causes sand streaking; congestion around rebar traps voids.

2. Where does rebar placement go wrong (insufficient cover, congestion, wrong lap length/location, displaced bars, no chairs), and how do builders guarantee correct placement and cover in the field? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE]
   Leads: cover controls corrosion life; laps located at low-stress points; chairs/spacers and tie wire to hold position during the pour.

3. What formwork failures do owner-builders hit (blowouts, bulging, misalignment, insufficient bracing, leaking joints), and what form design, tie spacing, and pour-rate controls prevent them? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [COST]
   Leads: fresh-concrete hydrostatic pressure rises with faster pour rate and colder mix; blowouts start at the base; snap-tie spacing and bracing.

4. What are the durability threats to reinforced concrete over a 100+ year life (rebar corrosion, carbonation, chloride ingress, sulfate attack, alkali-silica reaction, freeze-thaw), and what mix/cover/admixture choices maximize longevity? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
   Leads: air-entrainment for freeze-thaw; low w/c + adequate cover; supplementary cementitious materials (fly ash/slag) for sulfate/ASR resistance; epoxy/galvanized/stainless rebar in aggressive zones.

5. How do builders lay out movement/control joints and reinforcement in long concrete walls to control shrinkage cracking without compromising water-tightness? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

6. What creative structural-concrete options fit a durable owner-built home, and what are the buildability/cost tradeoffs (ICF, removable-form cast-in-place, post-tensioned slab, self-consolidating concrete, fiber reinforcement, low-carbon SCM-rich mixes)? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [COST] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
   Leads: SCC eliminates vibration and honeycomb risk; ICF combines forming + insulation; fibers reduce shrinkage cracking.

7. Which cast-in embed and connection problems cause the worst rework (misplaced anchor bolts, embed plates, sill anchorage, missed rebar dowels), and how do crews set and verify embeds before the pour? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

8. Given Oregon's Cascadia seismic risk, how are heavy masonry/concrete walls reinforced and anchored (bond beams, grouted vertical rebar, roof/floor diaphragm ties, hold-downs), and what detailing failures make mass walls dangerous in an earthquake? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE]
   Leads: unreinforced masonry is deadly in quakes; fully grouted reinforced masonry + bond beams + positive diaphragm-to-wall connection.

9. Is a low-cost frictional/sliding base-isolation system (as opposed to elastomeric rubber bearings) a realistic owner-builder-installable option under a concrete/masonry shell, and what does the foundation interface detail look like? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
   Leads: "low cost frictional seismic base-isolation of residential" research literature exists; mechanism and construction detail unverified.

10. What would it cost, per above-grade wall, to fully grout every cell (not just the reinforced cells required by ORSC R606 prescriptive) in this project's reinforced CMU walls, and what ductility or damage-reduction margin does the research literature attribute to full versus partial grouting? [CODE] [COST] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
   Leads: full grouting is standard West Coast seismic practice; most other U.S. seismic regions partially grout for cost; quantified performance delta between the two is a research gap.

11. Does wall density (shear-wall area as a percentage of floor plan area) as a design-check concept, used in confined-masonry seismic design internationally, have a directly applicable calculation for reinforced CMU/concrete residential design, and what wall-density ratio would this project's floor plan need to exceed to meaningfully outperform the code minimum? [METHOD] [CODE]
   Leads: Chilean confined-masonry research cites roughly 1.15 percent wall density as a performance threshold; the figure is for a different structural system (confined masonry, not reinforced CMU) and is unverified for direct transfer.

12. What specific ductile-detailing upgrades (closer vertical bar spacing than code minimum, continuous rather than lap-spliced vertical reinforcement, joint reinforcement at every course instead of every other course, dowels at wall-to-bond-beam interfaces) are buildable by an owner-builder crew at first construction, and what does each cost per linear foot of wall over the code-minimum baseline already stated in Structural System and Concrete Design? [METHOD] [COST] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]

13. What does designing this project's structural shell to an Immediate-Occupancy-equivalent performance target (per the ASCE 41 performance-level framework, normally used to judge retrofits of existing buildings, not new construction) require in reinforcement, detailing, and cost above the code's Life-Safety-equivalent minimum, and is there a residential precedent anywhere for voluntarily building new construction to that higher bar? ## What Concrete Uniquely Enables: Designing With Mass

1. Which of the three earth-sheltering forms (elevational/single-slope, penetrational/full berm, atrium/courtyard) fits an Oregon hillside parcel with a south-facing slope best, and what does each cost in added excavation and retaining structure compared with the daylight-basement baseline already assumed in the Foundation, Basement, Slabs, And Concrete Placement chapter? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]
   Leads: Malcolm Wells's three-form classification (elevational, penetrational, atrium); UNVERIFIED which Oregon jurisdictions have built precedent for any of the three.

2. What is the actual design dead load, in psf per inch of soil cover, for saturated winter soil on a bermed wall or an earth-covered roof, and how does it stack against the roof and wall design loads already tabulated in the Structural System And Concrete Design chapter? [CODE] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
   Leads: one builder's account cites roughly 10 psf per inch of saturated soil and a combined design load near 185 psf for a specific earth-covered structure (Earthwood); UNVERIFIED, needs an engineering source rather than a builder's own account.

3. How does Mononobe-Okabe seismic earth-pressure analysis change the reinforcement and thickness of a bermed exterior wall in Oregon Seismic Design Category D0/D1, compared with the ordinary basement-wall seismic provisions already summarized in the Foundation, Basement, Slabs, And Concrete Placement chapter? [CODE] [METHOD]
   Leads: Mononobe-Okabe pseudo-static analysis is the commonly cited seismic lateral-earth-pressure method, with safety factors against sliding and overturning reportedly dropping from about 1.5 static to about 1.1 seismic; UNVERIFIED how ORSC/ASCE 7 requires it applied to a habitable bermed wall specifically.

4. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
   Leads: hot-applied rubberized asphalt and bentonite/HDPE composite panels are the systems most cited for permanent burial; UNVERIFIED long-term (30-plus year) field data.

## Designing for Centuries: Durability Engineering

1. What does ACI PRC-201.2-23 (Guide to Durable Concrete) state, chapter by chapter, and does its guidance conflict anywhere with ACI 318 Chapter 19's exposure-class minimums for a project deliberately targeting beyond-code service life? [CODE]
   Leads: ACI 201.2R-23 organizes by mass transport then per-mechanism chapters; verify against ACI 318-19/22 directly.

2. How does the Eurocode 2 / BS 8500 "design working life" and "structural class" system translate cover and mix requirements as design life increases from 50 to 100 years, and can that adjustment logic be extrapolated (with engineering judgment, not code authority) toward a longer target? [CODE]
   Leads: BS 8500 Table A.4 (50-year) vs Table A.5 (100-year); structural class tied to concrete strength class and cover.

3. Has fib Model Code 2010's full-probabilistic durability design methodology ever been applied to a residential-scale structure, and is the underlying calculation (e.g., a chloride-diffusion service-life spreadsheet) something a structural engineer of record could run for this specific house? [CODE] [METHOD]

4. What does ACI 318's own commentary say about the assumed service life behind its exposure-class minimums (commonly understood as roughly 50-75 years), and how much conservative margin is available by applying the "severe" exposure class by default across the whole structural shell rather than only where triggered? [CODE]

5. Do any published case studies exist of a single-family residence (anywhere in the world) explicitly engineered to a stated multi-century design life, and what design choices did that project make? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

6. What is the actual engineering and regulatory reasoning behind the 500-year barrier-integrity requirement for low-level radioactive waste containment, and which of its conservative-design principles (cover, density, redundancy, monitoring) transfer to a residential structural shell versus which are containment-specific and irrelevant here? [CODE] [METHOD]
   Leads: unverified; low-level waste facility guidance reportedly requires 500-year intruder-barrier integrity with passive performance after year 100.

7. What do the geopolymer-concrete waste-containment projections of multi-millennial service life rest on (accelerated testing, modeling, or real long-term data), and is any of that testing methodology adaptable to verify a durability claim for this project's own alternative-materials test-to-prove path? [TEST-TO-PROVE] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
   Leads: unverified 2,500-5,000-year geopolymer projection figure, modeled/accelerated, not observed.

8. What carbonation depth, after how many years, is plausible for this project's specified long-life mix (0.45 or 0.40 w/c, with SCM) under Oregon's wet marine and inland climate conditions, using a recognized prediction model? [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

9. Is there a real risk that a high-fly-ash-replacement mix carbonates faster per unit depth than a plain portland mix if curing is cut short, and if so what replacement rate and curing duration combination avoids it? [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

10. Which specific locations on this house (garage slab, driveway-adjacent wall, any below-grade wall near a road, any surface exposed to wind-driven salt if coastal) carry meaningful chloride exposure risk in an inland or Cascades-foothill Oregon site, versus a genuinely coastal site, and how does the exposure-class assignment change between the two? [METHOD] [CODE]

   Leads: reported range 0.15-0.20% by mass of cement; verify against a single authoritative source before citing.

13.
