Is an embedded corrosion-monitoring sensor (a wireless resistivity or half-cell sensor cast into the concrete at construction time) a realistic, affordable addition for THIS project at the time of the original pour, and if so, at which specific locations (the highest-risk elements identified in section K above) does it earn its cost against periodic external testing alone, done later? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [TOOLS] [COST]

42. Does the specific build site's distance from the coast put it in any measurable wind-driven salt-spray exposure zone, and if the answer is no (an inland site), does that fully rule out chloride exposure as a design driver, or do the self-inflicted sources (deicing salt, any calcium chloride accelerator use) still matter regardless of distance from the ocean? [METHOD]

43. Does Oregon's building department review process (per the manuscript's existing permitting chapters) treat a durability-driven design choice (upgraded cover, corrosion-resistant rebar, a crystalline admixture) as a substitution requiring extra documentation, or as a straightforward exceeds-minimum design choice needing no special approval? [CODE] [METHOD]

44. Laid out side by side with cost, service life, and where-it-earns-its-cost for each: black steel, epoxy-coated, hot-dip galvanized, solid stainless, stainless-clad, GFRP, and basalt FRP, what single comparison table would let the owner choose per-element rather than researching each material separately? [COST] [METHOD]

45. What would a genuinely belt-and-suspenders "maximum durability" specification look like for this project's single highest-consequence, hardest-to-repair element (likely the footing and foundation stem wall), combining the best affordable option from mix design, SCM, cover, reinforcement type, and detailing all at once, and what would that cost against the baseline long-life spec already in the structural chapter? [METHOD] [COST]

46. Does UHPC (ultra-high-performance concrete) make sense for any single, small, high-consequence element on this project (a critical footing, a water cistern, a bond beam at a stress concentration) even if it is not practical for the whole structural shell, given its reported sub-100-year-plus service life and low permeability, and is it available in a small-batch or precast form an owner-builder could obtain? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [COST]

47. Across every durability technology gathered in this research (SCMs, corrosion-resistant rebar, crystalline admixtures, cathodic protection, sealers, detailing), which ones are genuinely additive (worth stacking together) and which are redundant (two ways of solving the same specific threat, where specifying both wastes money without adding protection)? [METHOD] [COST]

48. Does reduced wall wetting from a deeper overhang measurably reduce freeze-thaw cycling and inward vapor drive at the exterior concrete or masonry face, in a way that changes the durability assumptions in Designing for Centuries: Durability Engineering? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

## Passive and Bioclimatic Design

1. What is the actual solar-noon altitude at the winter and summer solstice for this project's specific candidate parcel latitude, computed and verified rather than assumed from a generic Willamette Valley figure? [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
   Leads: winter noon altitude approximately 90 minus latitude minus 23.5 degrees, summer approximately 90 minus latitude plus 23.5 degrees; unverified computation for a 44-45 degree latitude gives roughly 21-22 degrees winter, 68-69 degrees summer.

2. Which sun-path analysis tool or method (Solar Pathfinder, Solmetric SunEye, a printed sun-peg chart, or a free web sun-path tool) is the right fit for a mostly-solo owner-builder doing the site's own solar-access survey, and what does each actually cost and require in skill? [TOOLS] [COST] [METHOD]
   Leads: Solar Pathfinder roughly $300-500, analog, works in any weather; University of Oregon's Baker Lighting Lab keeps a 44-degree-north sun-peg chart, a real Oregon-calibrated resource worth contacting directly.

3. What NOAA or NWS station data exists for the actual candidate site's diurnal (day-night) summer temperature swing, and does it support night-flush cooling as a real strategy for this specific site, as opposed to a general Willamette Valley assumption? [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

4. What NREL NSRDB or PVWatts solar-resource data exists for the exact candidate parcel coordinates, replacing the generic statewide and Portland-area sun-hour figures found in general web sources? [METHOD]

5. How much does actual measured winter cloud cover and sun-hour data for the Willamette Valley (a verified NOAA climate normal, not a marketing-site summary) undercut the DOE passive-solar glazing-ratio guideline, and what glazing ratio does that data actually support? [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
   Leads: general sources report over 80 percent average winter cloud cover and 4-5 winter sun-hours per day for the valley, unverified against a primary NOAA source.

6. What is the prevailing wind direction for the Willamette Valley by season (not just an annual average), and how does it change the siting of operable windows for cross ventilation versus the siting of a windbreak for winter wind protection? [METHOD]

7. What building footprint and aspect ratio actually balances this project's two competing goals: a compact, low-surface-area form that minimizes total heat loss (the Passive House priority) against an elongated east-west form that maximizes south-wall area for solar gain (the classic passive-solar priority)? [METHOD] [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE]
   Leads: one source cites an optimum elongation near 1:1.3 along the east-west axis, unverified; Passive House form-factor targets run at or below about 0.8 per meter surface-to-volume, unverified.

8. Does a two-story or partial-basement massing change the surface-to-volume calculation enough to change the compact-versus-elongated recommendation for this specific house program, given the project's daylight-basement and multi-story options already discussed in the foundation chapter? [METHOD]

9. How does a masonry or concrete gable end, versus a hip roof, change shading self-obstruction on east and west walls, and does the roof form chosen for structural or aesthetic reasons in the structural and wall-system chapters accidentally help or hurt the passive design? [METHOD]

10. What does the actual primary source, DOE/NREL's "Passive Solar Design Strategies: Guidelines for Home Building, Eugene, Oregon" volume, state for this exact climate, and how does it reconcile with the generic 7-12 percent glazing rule found in secondary summaries? [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
   Leads: the document exists at a nrel.gov URL that this research pass could not fetch due to a network restriction; retrieve directly, by download or an alternate mirror, before writing the chapter.

11. For this house's actual floor area and room program, what south-glazing area (in square feet, not just a percentage) and what interior exposed-mass area does the DOE rule of thumb actually produce, run as a real worked calculation rather than left as an abstract percentage? [METHOD]

12. Given the direct disagreement found between DOE's rule-of-thumb glazing ratio (7-12 percent) and a practicing designer's contrarian claim (15-30 percent workable with active mass-charging fans), which position holds up against actual monitored-building data, and is there a monitored example in a comparably cloudy climate to Oregon's? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

13. Is a Trombe wall, built from the owner's own manufactured CMU or brick, a cost-justified option for this specific house, and what does it cost in dollars and in the wall's own detailing complexity against simply extending the standard continuous-exterior-insulation wall with a larger south-glazing area instead? [COST] [METHOD] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

14. What does a Trombe wall's air gap and vent detail need to be, specifically for an Oregon climate with mold and moisture risk in mind, given the gap is a sealed convective cavity that could trap condensation if not detailed correctly? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

15. What is a realistic, sourced construction-cost premium, in dollars per square foot, for a full direct-gain passive solar design (extra glazing plus extra exposed mass) over the project's current default wall and window specification, a number this research pass could not find? [COST]

16. Is an attached sunspace or solarium, sized to double as a plant-growing space, a better return for this owner's self-sufficiency goals than an equivalent-cost direct-gain glazing increase spread across ordinary rooms, and what does a sunspace actually cost to build onto a concrete house (2026)? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [COST] [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE]

17. What SHGC value, specifically, should the project specify for south-facing windows versus east- and west-facing windows, replacing the envelope chapter's current blanket statement that SHGC is an unconstrained design choice with an actual recommended value pair and its cost difference at the window order? [METHOD] [COST]
   Leads: general sources suggest higher SHGC (roughly 0.40-0.55) for south glazing, lower (below roughly 0.25-0.30) for east and especially west, unverified and needs a manufacturer-catalog and Oregon-climate-specific check.

18. What clerestory placement, sized against this house's actual room depths and ceiling heights, delivers usable daylight to the deepest rooms in the plan (an interior bathroom, a windowless hallway, a great room center) without the code-minimum 8 percent glazing already satisfying the requirement on paper but leaving the room visually dark? [METHOD]

19. Does a light shelf make sense on this project's south-facing windows given the already-specified deep overhangs and the interior finish palette, or does the overhang alone already block the shelf's own daylight-bounce function? [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

20. What window-to-wall ratio, room by room, actually fits this house's program, reconciling the 25-40 percent general daylighting optimum against the higher heat-loss cost of a concrete/masonry wall assembly where every additional square foot of glazing replaces a much better-insulated wall assembly? [METHOD] [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE]

21. Is a roof monitor a viable daylighting option on this project's specified roof assemblies (the protected-membrane roof or the wood-truss roof already detailed in the envelope chapter), and what does it cost in additional roof penetrations, structural framing, and waterproofing detail against a comparable skylight? [METHOD] [COST] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

22. What glare-control device (interior blind, exterior shade, light shelf, or a spectrally-selective coating) is actually most cost-effective at the specific window sizes and orientations this house is likely to use, priced against each other rather than discussed only in the abstract? [COST] [METHOD]

23. What visible transmittance (VT) value should this project target alongside its already-specified U-factor and SHGC values, since a window chosen purely for U-factor and SHGC can still deliver poor daylight quality? [METHOD]

24. What specific room-by-room operable-window layout achieves true cross ventilation in this house's actual floor plan, once one exists, rather than the generic "20 percent window rule" left as an abstraction? [METHOD]

25. Is a dedicated solar chimney (as opposed to relying on the existing roof form or an operable clerestory) worth the added construction cost and roof penetration for this specific house, given the stack-effect performance figures found are all from other climates and building types? [METHOD] [COST] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

26. How does the code-required whole-house balanced mechanical ventilation system (ORSC M1505, already specified in the HVAC chapter) interact with occupant-operated natural ventilation: does opening windows during a shoulder-season day upset the HRV/ERV's balance or efficiency, and should the system have an automatic window-open sensor cutout? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

27. What insect-screening and weather-sealing approach keeps a night-flush-sized operable opening (larger than a typical window vent) usable through an Oregon summer without admitting insects, wildfire smoke, or wind-driven rain during a sudden weather change? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]

28. During a wildfire-smoke event, already a real Pacific Northwest summer condition, how does the owner override the night-flush and natural-ventilation strategy back to sealed mechanical filtration without a manual, error-prone nightly decision? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

29. What CFM and air-change target does this specific house's floor area and exposed mass actually need for an effective night purge, replacing the generic 2-3 CFM per square foot rule with a number tied to this house's actual thermal mass and glazing load? [METHOD]

30. Is a powered whole-house fan, a passive stack-and-cross-vent-only strategy, or a hybrid the right choice for this project, weighed on installed cost, electrical load (relevant to the off-grid/battery sizing chapter), noise, and the security problem raised below? [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE] [COST] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

31. What is the actual, resolved answer to the night-flush-versus-security conflict for a rural Oregon owner-builder: lockable window restrictors, retractable security grilles, high clerestory-only openings, or a sealed house with a powered fan and secured low vents, priced and compared rather than left as an open list? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [COST] [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE]

32. Does exposing more interior concrete or CMU specifically to enable night flushing (rather than covering it with a finish material, an option the interiors chapter already allows) change the acoustic, cleaning, or comfort character of a room enough to matter, and has any real owner-builder documented that trade-off? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

33. What finish-floor covering choices (already limited by the radiant-floor R-value cap in the HVAC chapter) also happen to be compatible with, or actively hostile to, night-flush thermal mass exposure, since a covered slab cannot flush heat the same way an exposed one can? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

34. Which overhang-sizing method, the latitude-specific "overhang factor" table or the simpler universal 12-inch/18-inch rule, actually produces a better result for this project's specific latitude, and can this be resolved with an authoritative source rather than left as two competing unverified claims? [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

35. What vertical fin or louver (brise-soleil) design actually controls east and west glare and heat gain on this house, given fixed horizontal overhangs are established as ineffective for those orientations, and what does a fixed brise-soleil system cost against a comparable exterior roller shade? [METHOD] [COST]

36. Is an exterior roller shutter system (the standard European Passive House shading answer) available from a US or Pacific Northwest supplier at a reasonable cost and lead time for an owner-builder, or is it effectively a custom-import item for this project? [COST] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [TOOLS]

37. What Oregon-native or well-adapted deciduous tree species actually fit the passive-solar shading role (dense summer canopy, open winter branch structure, leaf-drop timing that matches the start of the local heating season), given the contrarian source's specific critique that branch structure and leaf-drop timing often do not match the textbook description? [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

38. What is the real-world maintenance and failure history of a pergola-plus-deciduous-vine shading system in a wet Oregon climate: does the wood or the vine structure rot, does the vine actually grow densely enough to shade effectively within a reasonable number of years, and what vine species are reported to work? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]

39. Given exterior shading is reported to outperform interior blinds by a wide margin (roughly 90-96 percent versus 70-85 percent of solar heat blocked), does that gap justify specifying exterior shading as the default for every south, east, and west window in this house, rather than treating interior blinds as adequate, and what is the added dollar cost of doing so across the whole house? [COST] [METHOD]

40. Can a motorized exterior shade or shutter be run on a simple sun-and-temperature-triggered automation, consistent with the project's local-first smart-home approach described in the smart-home chapter, without adding a cloud-dependent or subscription-dependent product to the house? [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

41. What is the actual, sourced relationship between radiant-floor surface temperature, mean radiant temperature, and a defensible lower thermostat setpoint for this house, replacing the unverified industry claim of "68 degrees F radiant feels like 72 degrees F forced air" with a real number or a real range? [TEST-TO-PROVE] [METHOD]

42. How does the ASHRAE 55 adaptive comfort model's extended temperature range for naturally-ventilated, occupant-controlled spaces apply specifically to this house's shoulder-season operation (natural ventilation and shading doing the comfort work, mechanical system not the sole driver), and should the HVAC chapter's thermostat setpoint schedule change seasonally to reflect it? [METHOD]

43. Does exposed interior concrete or CMU mass, at the surface temperatures this house's passive and radiant design would produce, ever create a mean-radiant-temperature discomfort in the other direction, an interior surface too cool in deep winter before the radiant floor has fully charged the room, and how is that cold-mass startup period managed? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

44. What are the actual, monitored performance numbers from the Salem, Oregon Passive House project referenced by PHIUS, and what does its documented design (glazing ratio, shading, mass strategy, actual measured overheating hours) suggest for this project, as the closest real local precedent found in this research pass? [TEST-TO-PROVE] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

45. Does the PHIUS+ climate-specific target for the Willamette Valley's climate zone exist as a published number, and how does it compare to this project's already-specified envelope performance (the Measure 7 blower-door and HRV targets in the envelope chapter)? [METHOD]

46. Where, specifically, does this project's concrete thermal mass earn its cost beyond what the already-specified superinsulated envelope alone would deliver: is it the multi-day outage-resilience benefit already described in the HVAC chapter, the night-flush summer-comfort benefit, the radiant-floor response-buffering benefit, or some combination, and can that be stated as a design decision with its basis rather than an unquantified assumption that "mass is good"? [METHOD] [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE]

47. Does the Passive House overheating criterion (indoor temperature above 25 degrees C for no more than 10 percent of occupied annual hours) apply usefully to this project as a design check, and if so, what combination of this project's own shading, glazing ratio, and night-flush strategy is needed to actually meet it, run as an explicit check rather than assumed? [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

48. What does the actual text of ORS 105.880 through 105.909 require to establish a solar easement across a neighboring parcel, what does it cost in legal fees to record one, and is it a realistic tool for a rural owner-builder buying raw land rather than a subdivision lot? [CODE] [COST] [NOT-RESEARCHABLE-PRO likely for the drafting step]

49. Does the county where the candidate parcel sits have a local solar access ordinance under ORS 227.190 or ORS 215.044, and if not, what does that absence mean in practice for the owner's ability to protect the house's winter solar access from future neighboring construction or tree growth? [CODE] [NOT-RESEARCHABLE-AHJ]

50. Does ORS 94.778's protection against HOA/CC&R solar-panel restrictions extend to passive-solar design elements (glazing area, orientation, roof form) as opposed to PV or solar-thermal hardware specifically, for any candidate parcel that turns out to sit inside a planned community or has recorded CC&Rs? [CODE] [NOT-RESEARCHABLE-PRO]

51. What defensible, Oregon-climate-calibrated rule-of-thumb figure exists (or can be derived) for the heat-pump tonnage, and therefore dollar cost, a well-executed passive design removes from the Manual J load, replacing the generic and contested national figures found in this research pass? [METHOD] [COST] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

52. What Oregon-native or well-adapted evergreen species and what specific spacing and distance-from-house guidance actually work as a winter windbreak for a Willamette Valley or high-desert site, replacing the generic "2-5 times mature height" rule found in this research pass with an Oregon-specific figure? [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD]

53. Does a windbreak sized to protect the house from winter wind also risk blocking useful summer cross-ventilation breeze from the same general direction, and how is that trade-off actually resolved on a real site rather than left as an abstract tension? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE]

54. For a high-desert eastern Oregon site (climate zone 5B), does a small courtyard water feature or evaporative element make a measurable comfort difference in the dry summer heat, and what does it cost against its water-use implication for a project with a stated water-independence goal? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [COST] [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE]

55. What outbuilding or landscape element placement (a detached shop, a garden wall, a berm) can double as a wind-block or a summer-shade device for the house itself, combining the site-development work already flagged under "Developing the Site" with this chapter's bioclimatic goals rather than treating them as separate projects? [CREATIVE-IDEA]

56. Is a small operable roof monitor or cupola, doing double duty as a daylighting device and a stack-effect ventilation outlet, buildable on this project's specified roof assemblies (protected-membrane concrete deck or wood-truss roof) at a reasonable cost, combining two of this chapter's strategies into one built element? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD] [COST]

57. Does a scaled-down windcatcher-like element (a small operable tower or vent stack, without the traditional evaporative-cooling pond) offer any real benefit in Oregon's marine climate, or is the humidity difference from its arid-climate origin enough to make it not worth adapting? [CREATIVE-IDEA] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

58. What do actual owner-builder forums and off-grid community documentation (not architecture-firm marketing material) report as the real, lived problems with a DIY passive solar or Trombe-wall retrofit or new build: what broke, what overheated, what the builder would do differently? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

59. Given the project's own thousand-year structural-shell goal, does a Trombe wall's glazing (a component with a much shorter service life than the concrete behind it, similar to the roof membrane's planned-replacement logic already established in the envelope chapter) need its own planned-replacement detail, and has anyone documented a multi-decade-old Trombe wall's actual glazing replacement? [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

60. What is a practical, owner-executable way to verify the as-built passive design is performing as intended (a simple data-logging thermometer set in the exposed mass and at a representative room location, tracked through a full year) rather than assuming the design works because the sizing rules were followed? [TEST-TO-PROVE] [METHOD] [TOOLS]

61. Does the pre-cover photography and records discipline already established in the envelope chapter (photographing window rough openings, flashing steps) need a passive-design-specific addition, such as photographing shading-device sightlines or clerestory placement before the roof closes in, so a decision made at the design stage is actually verifiable as built? [METHOD]

62. What does a one-year post-occupancy check of the passive design actually look like in practice: tracking indoor temperature swing through a full heating and cooling season against the design assumptions, and what threshold result would tell the owner the shading or glazing needs a correction (adding a shade, planting a tree) after the fact? [TEST-TO-PROVE] [METHOD]

63. Once window and clerestory placement is fixed by Passive And Bioclimatic Design, what interior finish choices (ceiling and upper-wall color and reflectance) actually bounce that daylight deeper into a finished room, as a distinct, interiors-side design lever from the window-sizing decision itself? [METHOD] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

64. Once the glazing itself is sized and any exterior shading is designed elsewhere, what interior-finish-level glare control (a matte versus polished floor finish, sheer versus blackout interior window treatments) actually changes daylight glare inside a finished room? [METHOD] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]

65. What glazing area, orientation, and overhang design keeps an elevational (single-slope) earth-sheltered design from overheating in an Oregon summer, given that the exposed face is typically glazed heavily for daylight and solar gain? [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

66. Given that a monitored field study of four passive-solar sunspaces in Eugene, Oregon found that conventional thermal-mass design rules need substantial revision for a rainy, cloudy climate and favored smaller thermal mass with reduced ground contact, does a PAHS or annualized-geo-solar design make sense for a Willamette Valley or Oregon coastal site, or does the state's cloudy winter climate undercut the solar-gain input the whole system depends on? [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
   Leads: "Interpretation of passive solar field data with EnergyPlus models: Un-conventional wisdom from four sunspaces in Eugene, Oregon" (ScienceDirect); UNVERIFIED beyond an abstract-level description found in this pass.


Contains merged Wave 3 research questions for: Foundation, Basement, Slabs, and Concrete Placement; The Wall System, Foundation to Roofline; Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes; Masonry Craft: Laying and Stacking Block and Brick; Envelope, Waterproofing, Thermal Performance, and Radon; Roof System and Roof-Wall Junctions; Aesthetics and Architectural Expression.

Source: consolidated and de-duplicated from the 21 pre-research draft banks (`A_` through `U_`) in this folder, plus the relevant seed items from Section A of `research_questions_revision7.md` (re-tagged from old chapter numbers to the current 36-chapter topic names per `STRUCTURE_PLAN.md`). Questions only; nothing here is answered. `Leads:` lines are unverified starting points, not facts.

## Foundation, Basement, Slabs, and Concrete Placement

### From the blockmaking tools, molds, mixers, and curing research (draft B)

**1. The block mix is dry-cast/compacted, not the rodded plastic concrete ASTM C31 assumes — how do you make a REPRESENTATIVE test specimen from a zero-slump block mix so its strength result actually reflects the pressed block?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [TEST-TO-PROVE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: ASTM C31 requires vibration (not rodding) for slump ≤1 in and allows metal OR plastic molds — but a rodded/vibrated cylinder still won't match press-compacted density; the correlation is the open question.

**2. What reusable test-cylinder molds should the owner use or make — plastic vs steel, 4×8 vs 6×12 in, cost and sourcing, or a DIY mold — and how do you cap/grind/level the ends for a valid compression test?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [TOOLS] [COST] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
Leads: C31 permits any non-absorbent, non-reactive, shape-stable, leak-free mold (metal or plastic).

**3. When and how do you make flexural beam specimens (modulus of rupture) alongside block/cylinder production — mold size, casting/consolidation method, and whether an owner-builder even needs them?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [TEST-TO-PROVE] [TOOLS] [METHOD]

**4. How can an owner actually compression-test blocks and cylinders without a commercial lab — a DIY hydraulic load frame + calibrated gauge, a repurposed press, versus paying a local testing lab per break — with cost and calibration/accuracy caveats?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [TEST-TO-PROVE] [COST]

**5. How do you correlate small-cylinder strength to full-block strength and to compacted unit density, and what sampling plan (specimens per batch, per day) gives real confidence without wasting material?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [TEST-TO-PROVE] [METHOD]

### From the structure and envelope problems-and-solutions research (draft D)

**6. What are the leading causes of slab-on-grade cracking (plastic shrinkage, drying shrinkage, curling, restraint), and which control-joint spacing, timing, and reinforcement practices prevent random cracks?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: joints roughly 24-36x slab thickness, cut within 6-12 hrs; high w/c ratio + poor curing are prime culprits; curling from top-down drying gradient.

**7. How do experienced crews pour concrete in cold Oregon weather (below 40°F) without freeze damage — what mix adjustments, heating, blankets, and strength thresholds do they rely on?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: freezing in first 24 hrs can cost up to 50% of strength permanently; protect until ~500 psi; warm mix water, non-chloride accelerators near rebar, insulating blankets/straw, heated enclosures; subgrade above 32°F; hold ~55°F cure.

**8. What causes hot/rapid-drying pour problems (plastic shrinkage cracking, cold joints, flash set), and how is a large owner-builder pour sequenced and staffed to avoid them?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: evaporation retarders, fog spray, timing pours off peak heat, set-retarding admixtures, continuous placement to avoid cold joints.

**9. Which below-grade waterproofing systems fail most often and why (damp-proofing mistaken for waterproofing, membrane discontinuities at cold joints/penetrations), and what belt-and-suspenders detailing do pros use?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: fluid-applied vs peel-and-stick vs bentonite vs dimple drainage board; dimple board doubles as drainage + membrane protection; treat cold joints and penetrations as the leak points.

**10. How is a reliable capillary break and under-slab moisture control built (gravel base, poly vapor barrier placement, sealed penetrations), and what mistakes let slab moisture ruin finished floors years later?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: vapor retarder directly under slab vs sand-cushion debate; punctured/unlapped poly; the radon gas-permeable layer can double as the capillary break.

**11. What are the failure modes of the footing-to-stemwall-to-slab cold joints, and how do builders waterstop and key them so water can't track through?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: hydrophilic and PVC waterstops, keyways, crystalline admixtures at construction joints.

**12. For a long-life concrete home, what creative slab/foundation options suit rural Oregon and what are the tradeoffs (frost-protected shallow foundation, thickened-edge/raft slab, integral radiant tubing, sub-slab EPS/XPS insulation)?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [COST]
Leads: FPSF avoids deep footings for frost; sub-slab rigid foam pairs with radiant slabs.

**13. What integral/admixture waterproofing options for concrete exist as alternatives or supplements to membranes (crystalline admixtures, hydrophobic/PRAH admixtures, water repellents), and how reliable are they below grade and for water-retaining concrete?**
Lands in: Foundation and Concrete Placement. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: self-sealing crystalline admixtures for fine cracks; usually still paired with a membrane as belt-and-suspenders.

## The Wall System, Foundation to Roofline

### From the Wave 3 seed questions (Section A, the double-wythe path)

**1. Veneer versus structural wythe. For the owner's inner-CMU + cavity insulation + outer-brick wall: does TMS 402 treat the outer brick as an anchored masonry veneer (non-loadbearing, Chapter 12) or as a structural wythe of a composite/noncomposite multi-wythe wall (Chapter 5)? State what changes structurally, in code path, and in engineering requirement between the two readings.**
Lands in: The Wall System, Foundation to Roofline.

**2. Tie systems and schedules. Wall-tie and veneer-tie types (adjustable two-piece, wire, joint-reinforcement ladder/truss), spacing limits (TMS 402; IRC R703.8 for veneer), corrosion protection by exposure (hot-dip galvanized vs. stainless), and the thermal-bridging penalty of steel ties crossing the cavity insulation.**
Lands in: The Wall System, Foundation to Roofline.

**3. Cavity, drainage, and insulation. Minimum air space, the drainage plane, weep spacing and type, base flashing, and which cavity insulations suit a drained-and-vented cavity (rigid foam vs. mineral-wool board vs. injected).**
Lands in: The Wall System, Foundation to Roofline.

**4. Differential movement. Brick moisture expansion vs. CMU drying shrinkage: expansion joints in brick, control joints in CMU, spacing (BIA Technical Notes, NCMA/CMHA TEK), and how the ties accommodate movement.**
Lands in: The Wall System, Foundation to Roofline.

**5. Seismic in Oregon SDC D0/D1. Veneer and multi-wythe anchorage seismic requirements (TMS 402 seismic provisions), prescriptive limits, and where engineering becomes mandatory for the double-wythe.**
Lands in: The Wall System, Foundation to Roofline.

**6. Foundation for the double-wythe. Footing width and the brick-shelf/ledge detail to carry both wythes, the slab-edge thermal break at the wall base, and how this differs from the single-wythe default footing.**
Lands in: The Wall System, Foundation to Roofline; cross-reference Foundation, Basement, Slabs, and Concrete Placement.

### From the owner-made and alternative materials research (draft A)

**7. For interlocking dry-stack CEB (mortarless walls), how is a wall system proven structurally for permitting when the units interlock rather than bed in mortar, and what test stands in for a masonry prism?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [CODE] [TEST-TO-PROVE] [METHOD]
Leads: Hydraform interlocking dry-stack blocks; question is system-level acceptance, not unit strength alone.

**8. What mortar mix, cement content, sand spec, and mesh layup define structural ferrocement, and how thin can a loadbearing element go?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD]
Leads: cement:sand ~1:1.5 to 1:3, w/c 0.4–0.45, ~500–600 kg/m³ cement, mesh 0.5–1.0 mm wire at 5–10 mm spacing, elements 2–3 cm thick.

**9. Could ferrocement serve as stay-in-place formwork or a thin-shell skin that is then filled or backed with owner-made concrete, giving a hybrid wall — and how would that composite be tested?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]

**10. Weighing durability, seismic performance, makeability, and cost together, which owner-made materials are genuinely fit for a 100-year concrete home versus which are interesting but should stay out of the load path?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA] [COST]

### From the mortar, grout, and jointing research (draft C)

**11. Exactly which mortar type belongs in each part of this wall — the reinforced/loadbearing inner CMU wythe, the outer brick veneer, the below-grade/foundation courses, and any grouted bond beams — and what is the reasoning (compressive strength vs bond vs flexibility) behind each pick?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE]
Leads: Type S for reinforced/loadbearing CMU; Type N preferred for brick veneer (better bond/water resistance, more forgiving of movement); Type M or S often specified below grade / in earth contact.

**12. How do integral water-repellent mortar admixtures work, what are the real products and dose rates, and how much do they actually cut water absorption and efflorescence without killing bond or breathability?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [COST]
Leads: DRY-BLOCK Mortar Admixture (GCP/Chryso), Euclid Hydrapel, Master Builders/BASF Rheopel, SPEC MIX IWR; polymeric/stearate chemistry; meant to pair with an IWR block admixture.

**13. What is the real difference between mortar and grout (and why is neither ordinary concrete), why must grout be a very fluid 8–11 in slump while mortar is stiff, and what goes wrong if the owner packs cores with leftover stiff mortar instead of grout?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: ASTM C476; the high slump lets grout flow around rebar and bond to it while the dry block sucks the excess water back out; stiff mortar leaves voids around steel.

**14. When is fine grout required versus coarse grout (ASTM C476), how is the choice driven by cell/cavity size and rebar congestion, and what are the proportions of each?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [CODE]
Leads: fine (fine aggregate only) for tight/congested cells; coarse adds pea-gravel for larger cells; C476 by proportion or by 28-day strength at 8–11 in slump.

**15. What are the maximum grout lift and pour heights, when is consolidation (puddling/vibration) and reconsolidation required, and how do owner mistakes here create voids, blowouts, or grout that shrinks away from the block?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE]
Leads: consolidate lifts >12 in; high-lift/story-height pours allowed with conditions (unit age ~4 hr, slump ~10–11 in, no intermediate bond beams); reconsolidate as grout settles/shrinks.

**16. Is self-consolidating grout (SCG) a smart option for a solo owner-builder who can't reliably vibrate every lift — what slump-flow and visual-stability index does it need, and what are the cost and segregation risks?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [COST] [METHOD]
Leads: SCG slump flow ~24–30 in, VSI ≤1, no mechanical consolidation; higher material cost; segregates if mis-proportioned.

**17. How much does grout shrink as it cures, why must it carry excess water to flow (which the unit then reabsorbs), and what technique prevents the shrinkage gap that would let water track straight down a grouted core?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

**18. Thermal and moisture movement cracking: how much do clay brick (irreversible long-term moisture expansion + thermal) and concrete block (drying shrinkage + thermal) actually move, why do they move in opposite directions, and how does that differential tear a rigid double-wythe wall that lacks the right joints?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: brick expands permanently over years plus thermal; CMU shrinks as it dries plus thermal — opposite signs are the whole reason expansion joints (brick) and control joints (block) are different things.

**19. Efflorescence: what are the three required ingredients (soluble salts + moisture + a migration path), what distinguishes primary "new building bloom" from recurring secondary efflorescence, and what are the concrete prevention levers — low-alkali cement, IWR admixture, flashing/weeps, keeping units dry and covered on site?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: salts + water + path; new building bloom largely from mix/rain water in fresh work; low-alkali ASTM C150 cement, IWR admixtures, drainage detailing, cover stored units; dry-brush before wet-wash for removal.

**20. Peeling and blistering of surface coatings: why do film-forming paints/sealers peel and spall masonry in a freezing climate by trapping vapor, and what is the vapor-permeable alternative (penetrating silane/siloxane), including its limits (don't seal a wet or salt-laden wall)?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: film coatings trap vapor → freeze → spall/peel; breathable silane/siloxane penetrants react with silica in the pores and don't form a film; sealing traps existing moisture if applied wrong.

**21. Sulfate attack below grade: how do sulfates in Oregon soils/groundwater attack ordinary portland mortar (expansive ettringite), and when does the owner need Type II or Type V (low-C3A) cement, a denser low-w/c mix, and parging/damp-proofing on the below-grade wythe?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [CODE]
Leads: Type II C3A ≤8% (moderate), Type V C3A ≤5% (severe); low w/c dense mortar matters as much as cement type; parge + damp-proof below grade.

**22. "Mortar dropping" bridging the cavity: how do droppings and "snots" on ties bridge the air gap and pipe water across to the inner wythe, and what site technique keeps a double-wythe cavity clean — beveling the bed away from the cavity, drawing a cavity board, mortar-catch mesh, and a minimum clear airspace?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: droppings piling on flashing defeat the weeps; bevel beds, draw a batten/board up the cavity, Mortar Net-type deflection; BIA asks ~2 in minimum clear cavity.

**23. Expansion joints (brick, which expands) vs control joints (CMU, which shrinks): what is the physical difference, how is each built (compressible filler + sealant vs bond-breaker/weakened plane + backer rod + sealant), and why does using the wrong one — or none — guarantee cracking?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: brick expansion joint stays open/compressible to close as brick grows; CMU control joint is a deliberate weak plane meant to open as block shrinks; BIA vs NCMA/CMHA (TEK) govern the two.

**24. What spacing and placement rules govern brick expansion joints and CMU control joints (BIA expansion-joint formula, NCMA/CMHA control-joint criteria, plus joints at openings, corners, offsets, and under shelf angles), and how should the owner lay them out before the first course goes down?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [METHOD] [CODE]
Leads: BIA expansion-joint formula lands around ~23 ft typical; NCMA/CMHA TEK ~25 ft max control-joint spacing and at openings; horizontal expansion joints under shelf angles.

**25. Flashing and weeps in a drainage cavity wall: where does through-wall flashing go (base of wall, sills, heads, shelf angles), how are end dams formed, and how are weeps sized and spaced (roughly 16–24 in o.c.) and kept open so water that enters actually drains back out?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE]
Leads: weeps placed directly above flashing at 16–24 in o.c.; end dams at terminations; flash every interruption; the drainage-wall premise — water WILL get in and must get out.

**26. How does the owner tell an active (still-moving) crack from a dormant one, and choose the right fix — repoint, route-and-seal with elastomeric sealant, or recognize that the crack is really a missing movement joint rather than a "mortar problem"?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**27. How does the owner run and read a test panel / mock-up for water penetration (e.g., an ASTM E514-style wall spray) and for appearance, tooling, and drainage before that detail is built into the finished home?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [TEST-TO-PROVE] [METHOD]
Leads: E514 wall spray test; visual/tooling review; confirm weeps actually drip.

**28. What is the cost/benefit over a 100-year wall life of the durability upgrades (IWR admixture, mortar cement vs masonry cement, Grade SW brick, breathable penetrating sealer, extra flashing/weeps) — which ones pay for themselves in avoided repointing and spalling repair?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [COST] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**29. What does the Oregon Residential Specialty Code (and its referenced masonry standards, TMS 402/602) require for mortar type, grout, ties/joint reinforcement, cold/hot-weather protection, and movement joints in an owner-built masonry wall — and what triggers engineering or special inspection?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [CODE]
Leads: verify through `revision6_code_library` routes; render as three-part Code entries (requirement / exact value / source line); ORSC references TMS 402-602 masonry provisions.

**30. What are Oregon's cold/wet-climate constraints on this work — when Grade SW brick is required (weathering index), the temperature thresholds at which masonry work must stop, heat, or be protected, and how wet-season saturation drives the drainage-wall detailing differently in the wet Willamette valley vs the high-desert freeze-thaw east side?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [CODE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: Grade SW where weathering index ≥50; cold-weather protection commonly triggered around 40°F and falling (TMS 602 / IMI); saturation + freeze is the spalling driver.

### From the structure and envelope problems-and-solutions research (draft D)

**31. What are the classic moisture failures of a double-wythe cavity wall (mortar droppings bridging the cavity, blocked or missing weeps, no through-wall flashing, saturated inner wythe), and what cavity-clean, flashing, and weep details keep water off the inner wythe?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: keep the ~2" cavity clear; mortar-catch devices / beveled bed joints; through-wall flashing + weeps at base and every shelf angle/opening; drainage mat over droppings.

**32. Why do masonry wall ties corrode and fail, which tie materials and spacing keep the outer wythe from bulging/cracking, and how are ties detailed so they don't conduct water inward?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [CODE]
Leads: galvanized ties fail where galvanizing is thin and porous outer brick lets water in; horizontal cracks appear at regular tie intervals as ties rust and expand; stainless ties for long life; drip formed in the tie so water doesn't bridge.

**33. How is differential movement between a clay-brick outer wythe (moisture expansion + thermal growth) and a CMU/concrete inner wythe (drying shrinkage) accommodated, and what happens when expansion/control joints are omitted?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: brick grows while concrete/CMU shrinks — opposite movements; vertical expansion joints in brick vs control joints in CMU; soft joints under shelf angles; restraint causes cracking and spalling.

**34. What thermal-bridging and condensation problems arise in a masonry cavity wall (ties, shelf angles, uninsulated cavity, wrong insulation in a wet cavity), and how is cavity insulation kept dry and continuous?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: use closed-cell/rigid board that tolerates cavity moisture; insulate against the inner wythe leaving a clear drainage gap; thermal-break ties.

**35. How does solar-driven inward vapor drive from a rain-saturated brick/masonry outer wythe cause interior moisture, and what venting/cavity/vapor-control strategy stops it — especially where interior polyethylene is present?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: Lstiburek/BSC RR-0104 "solar-driven moisture in brick veneer"; wet brick is a reservoir, sun drives stored water inward; a mortar-free, top-and-bottom-vented drained cavity intercepts it; avoid interior poly on air-conditioned walls.

**36. Which flashing and drainage details at the cavity base, at openings, and at shelf angles are most often botched, and what sequence (flashing, drip edge, end dams, weeps) makes the cavity actually drain?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: end dams at jambs, flashing turned up behind the backup and lapped over the WRB, weeps roughly ≤24-33" o.c.

**37. For an Oregon marine (zone 4C) climate, how should the control layers of a double-wythe wall be ordered (drainage plane, air barrier, insulation, vapor control) so the assembly dries, and which orders trap moisture?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: "perfect wall" control-layer stack; air-tightness takes primacy over vapor barriers in 4C; smart vapor retarder inboard, vapor-open outboard, avoid a low-perm layer on the wrong side.

**38. What creative uses of the cavity itself are worth weighing (rain-screen drainage gap, continuous insulation zone, service chase, positioning thermal mass inside the insulation line), and what are the moisture tradeoffs of each?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [CREATIVE-IDEA]

**39. How do builders diagnose and repair a double-wythe wall that is already leaking or has failing ties without demolishing the outer wythe (helical retrofit ties, repointing, cavity clearing, retrofit weeps/flashing)?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [COST]
Leads: helical remedial wall ties; borescope cavity survey; retrofitted weeps.

**40. How are windows/doors integrated into a thick double-wythe masonry opening (buck details, where the unit sits in the wall depth relative to the insulation/air-barrier plane, sub-sill flashing draining to the cavity) without leaks or thermal bridges?**
Lands in: The Wall System. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: align the window with the control layers; masonry openings need through-wall flashing under the sill draining to weeps.

### From the aesthetics and architectural expression research (draft H)

**41. ## Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes

### From the owner-made and alternative materials research (draft A)

**1. What is the actual recipe of a "zero-slump / no-slump" dry-cast concrete — target water/cement ratio, aggregate gradation, and fines content — that holds its shape the instant the mold is lifted?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD]
Leads: NPCA "dry cast = zero/negative slump"; low w/b + high pressure is the mechanism, not chemistry.

**2. Which combination of static pressure and vibration frequency produces full compaction of a zero-slump mix, and can those parameters be reproduced at owner scale?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [METHOD] [TOOLS]
Leads: published paving-block work used ~75 kg/cm² pressure with 25–50 Hz vibration at low w/b.

**3. For an owner without a commercial block plant, which is more achievable for immediate demold — high static hydraulic pressing (no vibration) or vibration-plus-modest-pressure — and what press tonnage does each route need for a standard block footprint?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [METHOD] [TOOLS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**4. After immediate demold, what curing regime (moist cover, fog, steam, or sealed) brings a dry-cast unit from green strength up to a loadbearing target, and on what day-by-day schedule?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**5. What does a low-mold, high-throughput production line look like for one or two people making ~100 units/day — cycle time per unit, mold count, curing-yard layout, and unit handling before the material can bear its own stacking load?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [METHOD] [TOOLS] [COST]

**6. What cement percentage (by dry soil mass) does CSRE need to clear a chosen structural target, and what is the strength-vs-cement curve across the common 5% / 7% / 10% band?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
Leads: CSRE studies report ~3.3–4.07 MPa at 1 day and continued gain past 28 days; New Mexico earthen code floor is 300 psi (~2.07 MPa).

**7. How is the optimum moisture content for CSRE compaction found (standard/modified Proctor), and how tightly must field moisture track that optimum to hit target density and strength?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

**8. How much compaction energy does CSRE need, and what tool delivers it for a fast-demold pressed block — hand tamper vs pneumatic rammer vs hydraulic press — with the density-to-strength relationship for each?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [METHOD] [TOOLS]

**9. Can CSRE be run as a pressed, immediately demolded *block* (rather than monolithic in-wall rammed earth), and does it hold enough green strength at demold to be handled and stacked?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]

**10. How durable is CSRE in wet western/central Oregon — wet/dry cycling, freeze/thaw, and rain erosion — and what minimum cement content or surface treatment is needed for durability (which may exceed the content needed for strength alone)?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
Leads: ASTM D559 (wet-dry) and D560 (freeze-thaw) soil-cement durability tests; durability often governs cement dose over strength.

**11. Which cheap additives raise CSRE strength or durability without killing fast demold — hydrated lime, fly ash, a splash of sodium silicate, or short fibers — and at what dose does each help vs hurt?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

**12. How do the CEB press classes compare for an owner — manual CINVA-ram, mid-range manual/mechanical, and hydraulic machines — on compaction pressure, blocks/hour, resulting green strength, and price?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [TOOLS] [METHOD] [COST]
Leads: AECT Impact 2001A ~300 blocks/hr on a 7 HP diesel; Hydraform M7MI twin-head ~20 tons hydraulic pressure; manual CINVA-ram ~2 blocks/min with two people.

**13. Are there proven owner-built CEB/CSEB press plans (shop-fabricated hydraulic or lever presses), and what compaction pressure and block quality do home-built presses actually achieve?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [TOOLS] [CREATIVE-IDEA] [COST]

**14. Is a one-part "just add water" geopolymer (dry, pre-blended solid activator) practical for an owner, and what strength does it reach vs the messier two-part liquid-activator route?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: "one-part geopolymerisation" / just-add-water systems using solid alkaline reagents.

**15. What is a workable two-part activator recipe and its handling/safety protocol — NaOH molarity, sodium-silicate-to-hydroxide ratio, silicate modulus, mixing order, PPE, and heat of solution?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: reported binary activators ~10 M NaOH with Na2SiO3, NaOH/Na2SiO3 ~0.4, silicate modulus SiO2/Na2O ~1.31–1.43.

**16. How much ground-granulated slag must go into a fly-ash geopolymer to get ambient-cure early strength (avoiding the heat-cure that pure fly-ash systems need), and what 1-day and 28-day strengths result?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD]
Leads: ambient-cured Class F fly ash + slag bricks reported 16.7–25.3 MPa at 28 days; small slag fraction boosts early kinetics.

**17. Can a geopolymer binder be combined with dry-cast pressing to make a fast-demold pressed geopolymer block, and does the fresh compacted unit hold shape before the geopolymerization sets?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]

**18. What causes geopolymer efflorescence and how is it mitigated, and what is the long-term durability of ambient-cured geopolymer in a wet climate?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: slag inclusion / lower-silicate activator reduces efflorescence across curing regimes.

**19. How do natural hydraulic lime (NHL) grades — NHL 2, 3.5, 5 — differ in set time and strength, and can an owner reach a structural target with NHL plus pozzolan?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: NHL5 blended with silica fume + GGBS reported ~28 MPa 28-day cube strength.

**20. What lime-to-pozzolan ratio and mix procedure (including hot-mix / hot-lime methods) gives usable structural strength, and how long until it reaches it?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD]
Leads: Vitruvius' 2:1 pozzolana:lime recurring across historical mixes; ~9.49 MPa reported at 60 days.

**21. Can a lime-pozzolan mix be pressed and demolded green like dry-cast, or does its slow set make it incompatible with the ~100-units/day goal — and what hybrid (small cement or slag addition) fixes that?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

**22. What is the sulfur concrete mix and melt/mix procedure, and what are the real safety constraints (SO2/H2S fumes, ignition, hot handling) for an owner working at ~130–140 °C?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: typical ~20% sulfur / 32% fine / 48% coarse aggregate, sometimes ~5% silica flour; melt ~130–140 °C.

**23. How fast does sulfur concrete gain strength on cooling, and does its melt-and-freeze set make it the ultimate fast-demold, reusable-mold material?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]
Leads: reported ~90% of ultimate strength in 6–8 hours; ultimate ~40–70 MPa; touted as 100% recyclable (remelt).

**24. What steel-fiber dose and geometry meaningfully raise flexural strength and toughness of an owner mix, and does it change the compression-test result the permit relies on?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

**25. Is engineered "bendable concrete" (PVA-fiber ECC / strain-hardening cementitious composite) reproducible by an owner, what does it cost, and where would its ductility matter (seismic Oregon)?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [COST]

**26. For natural fibers (basalt, sisal, jute, coir), what dose helps vs hurts compressive strength, and how is the long-term fiber-rot / alkaline-degradation problem solved (treatment or pozzolan matrix)?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: natural fibers typically 0.25–1.5%; silane-treated sisal mix reported ~32 MPa; untreated natural fiber degrades in cement alkalinity.

**27. How do accelerators compare for an owner — calcium chloride vs non-chloride (calcium nitrate/formate) — on dose, early-strength gain, and downsides (shrinkage, corrosion, discoloration)?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: CaCl2 dosed ~1–2% by cement mass (2% ceiling; corrosion/shrinkage risk); non-chloride ~0.5–2% or ~10–45 fl oz per 100 lb cement.

**28. How much do Type III (high-early) cement, a lower water/cement ratio, and a warmer mix each buy in demold-time strength, and can an owner stack them?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD]

**29. What owner-scale steam or hot-box curing setup (insulated chamber, temperature, humidity, schedule) gets same-day or next-day strength without cracking or delayed-ettringite problems?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [METHOD] [TOOLS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**30. Is CO2 carbonation curing feasible at owner scale for dry-cast blocks (a chamber flooded with combustion or bottled CO2), and what strength and speed does it add?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]
Leads: accelerated carbonation of CMUs added ~15% (7-day) to ~28% (28-day) strength, most gain between 4–24 hours, after 50–60% RH preconditioning.

### From the mortar, grout, and jointing research (draft C)

**74. For owner-made CMU/blocks, does an integral-water-repellent block system require a matching integral-water-repellent mortar admixture to actually work, and what happens to bond and efflorescence if the block and mortar water-repellent systems are mismatched?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: DRY-BLOCK = block admixture + mortar admixture as a paired system; mismatch breaks water-repellent continuity right at the joint line.

**75. Can pozzolans (fly ash, metakaolin, natural pozzolan) or natural hydraulic lime replace part of the portland to make a more flexible, breathable, self-healing, sulfate-resistant joint — and what mix and schedule consequences (slower strength gain, longer cure protection) must the owner plan for?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: NHL 3.5 self-heals via free lime, is more vapor-permeable than any portland mix, has a plate-crystal structure that flexes and needs fewer control joints; pozzolans improve sulfate resistance and cut permeability.
