**141. What curing-rack design (material, spacing, stackability) actually holds up to a roughly 100-unit-per-day production pace over the weeks-to-months this project's block campaign will run, and what do owner-builders who have run a similar-scale block operation report building or buying?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [TOOLS] [METHOD] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

**142. Wood, plastic, or steel pallets under cast block: what does each actually cost per unit at the quantity this project needs (enough pallets in rotation to support a 100-unit-per-day cure cycle), and which material actually survives repeated wetting and reuse best in Oregon's wet-season conditions?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [COST] [TOOLS]

**143. Beyond wire brushing and pressure washing, what mold-release product and reapplication schedule actually keeps a block or paver mold casting cleanly at a roughly 100-unit-per-day pace without a buildup-related dimensional-tolerance problem developing over a multi-week production run?**
Lands in: Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]


## Masonry Craft: Laying and Stacking Block and Brick

### From the Wave 3 seed questions (Section A, the double-wythe path)

**1. Mortar (ASTM C270). Mortar types M/S/N/O, which for loadbearing CMU, for brick veneer, and below grade; proportion vs. property spec; mixing, board life, and retempering rules.**
Lands in: Masonry Craft: Laying and Stacking Block and Brick.

**2. Grout (ASTM C476). Fine vs. coarse grout, slump, lift heights, consolidation and reconsolidation, low-lift vs. high-lift grouting, and cleanout requirements.**
Lands in: Masonry Craft: Laying and Stacking Block and Brick.

**3. Laying technique and tolerances. Coursing, bed and head joints, story poles, leads and lines, TMS 602 dimensional tolerances, plumb/level control, and joint tooling profiles (concave, V) and when each is used.**
Lands in: Masonry Craft: Laying and Stacking Block and Brick.

**4. Weeps, flashing, and drainage at the craft level. Through-wall flashing materials and lap detailing, weep spacing and type (open head joint, rope, vented), and mortar-dropping control (mortar net).**
Lands in: Masonry Craft: Laying and Stacking Block and Brick.

**5. Cold- and hot-weather masonry. TMS 602 construction and protection procedures by temperature band, admixtures, and heating/protection methods.**
Lands in: Masonry Craft: Laying and Stacking Block and Brick.

**6. Reinforcement placement in masonry. Vertical bar positioning and cover in cells, horizontal joint reinforcement vs. bond-beam bars, and lap splices in grout.**
Lands in: Masonry Craft: Laying and Stacking Block and Brick; cross-reference Structural System and Concrete Design.

### From the mortar, grout, and jointing research (draft C)

**7. What are the exact volumetric proportions of portland cement, hydrated lime, and sand for each mortar type (M, S, N, O) under the ASTM C270 proportion specification, and how does an owner batch these accurately by shovel/bucket on site without a scale (and without drift as the pile settles)?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD] [CODE]
Leads: C270 proportion spec is the default when neither method is named; Type N cement-lime ≈ 1 cement : ½–1¼ lime : sand at 2¼–3× the cement+lime volume.

**8. What is the difference between the C270 "proportion specification" and "property specification," why is proportion the default and the safer path for an owner-builder, and what situation would force a switch to property (lab pre-testing of the actual mix)?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD] [CODE]
Leads: property spec requires lab compressive strength, water retention, and air content on the mix before use; proportion just fixes the recipe.

**9. What does hydrated lime actually contribute (workability, water retention, autogenous self-healing, bond, breathability) versus what portland cement contributes (early strength, set), and how does shifting the lime:cement ratio let the owner "tune" a joint's hardness and permeability to the brick?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: lime raises water retention/workability, re-crystallizes to heal microcracks, softens the joint; under proportion spec the lime volume can legally range ½–1¼ per volume of cement to match unit IRA.

**10. What sand gradation does ASTM C144 require (sieve limits, fineness modulus), and how do too-fine, too-coarse, gap-graded, or dirty (silt/clay/organic) sands each cause a specific defect — harsh unworkable boards, high water demand, drying-shrinkage cracks, weak bond, or staining?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
Leads: C144 sieve limits; sand is filler that controls shrinkage; well-graded sand needs less water and paste; a jar/sieve field test can screen a sand pile.

**11. How much water is right, and what exactly is "retempering" — how long is a mortar board alive (board life), how much re-wetting restores workability without wrecking strength/bond, and when must a batch be discarded (after initial set / past ~2½ hr)?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: retempering to replace evaporated water is allowed within board life (~2½ hr); once hydration set begins, re-wetting destroys bond — throw it out.

**12. What is the difference among masonry cement (ASTM C91), mortar cement (ASTM C1329), and field-mixed portland-lime (PCL), and why does mortar cement deliver higher flexural bond than masonry cement — which should the owner choose for the veneer vs the block, and why?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [CODE]
Leads: masonry cement carries proprietary additives + entrained air and has no minimum bond requirement; mortar cement (C1329) has a minimum flexural bond and capped air; PCL is fully owner-controlled.

**13. What mix water is acceptable (potable, no seawater or high-sulfate/organic water), what temperature should sand and water be on hot vs cold days, and how do dirty or salty water and unwashed sand seed efflorescence and tie corrosion later?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**14. Why is the strongest mortar (Type M) usually the wrong choice for brick veneer even though "stronger" sounds safer — how does an over-rigid, high-cement mortar concentrate stress and crack the units instead of yielding at the joint?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: over-hard mortar forces movement into the brick face → spalling; the joint is meant to be the weaker, sacrificial, repairable element.

**15. What is the "sacrificial joint" principle — why must the mortar always be no harder and no less vapor-permeable than the masonry unit — and how does violating it (hard portland mortar on soft or owner-made brick) cause face spalling, trapped moisture, and premature unit failure?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: mortar should deteriorate and be repointed before the brick does; portland's low permeability traps water, then freeze-thaw spalls the brick face.

**16. What are the families of mortar admixtures and what single property does each change — plasticizers/workability aids, air-entrainers, retarders, accelerators, integral water repellents, bonding polymers, pigments, pozzolans — and which are genuinely worth it for an owner-builder versus marketing?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

**17. When (if ever) should an air-entraining admixture go into mortar for freeze-thaw durability, and why does the industry warn that entrained air much above ~12–14% sharply lowers compressive AND bond strength — how does the owner weigh freeze-thaw durability of the mortar against bond to the unit?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: entrained air helps mortar survive freeze-thaw but lowers bond; masonry cement already carries air; C270 property spec caps air content by type.

**18. What non-chloride accelerators are safe in cold-weather mortar, why is calcium chloride (and every "antifreeze") prohibited by the masonry code, what corrosion does it drive in ties/anchors/joint reinforcement, and what does an accelerator actually do (and not do)?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE]
Leads: CaCl2 banned (corrodes embedded metal); use calcium nitrite/nitrate non-chloride accelerators; accelerators speed hydration/early strength — they are NOT antifreeze and don't remove the need to protect from freezing.

**19. What do set retarders / extended-life mortars do for hot-weather work or a long board, and what are the trade-offs in early strength and bond if a retarded joint is tooled or loaded before it has really set?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD]

**20. When is a bonding agent / polymer (acrylic latex, SBR, EVA redispersible powder) worth adding to mortar or brushing on as a slurry bond coat, which real products exist, and where does it genuinely help versus just add cost?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [CREATIVE-IDEA] [COST]
Leads: SikaLatex R, W.R. Meadows Acry-Lok, ChemMasters Cretelox; latex raises bond/flexural/freeze-thaw and cuts permeability; Type 1 re-emulsifiable vs Type 2 not; ~30-min tack window at 75°F.

**21. How are pigments correctly added to colored mortar (iron oxide vs carbon black), why does carbon black fade/leach outdoors while iron oxides stay UV- and alkali-stable, what is the dose ceiling as a percent of cementitious, and how does batch-to-batch color inconsistency itself become a visible defect?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: iron oxide UV/alkali-stable; carbon black can leach and is not recommended exterior; pigment dosed as % of cement, tight repeatable batching to avoid streaks/panels.

**22. Which traditional/folk "additives" do masons still mention — dish soap for air, sugar as a retarder, table salt as "antifreeze," fireclay for workability — and exactly how does each quietly damage bond, durability, or embedded steel so the owner knows why to avoid them?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: soap = uncontrolled air/low bond; sugar = unpredictable/over-retardation; salt = corrosion + efflorescence; document the harm mechanism, not just "don't."

**23. Shrinkage/drying cracking: what actually causes plastic and drying-shrinkage cracks in mortar joints (too much water, too much cement, too-fine sand, fast drying, missing control joints), and what is the specific prevention for each cause?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**24. Spalling and "popping": distinguish freeze-thaw spalling of saturated units, lime popping/pitting from unslaked or over-burnt lime particles, and aggregate pop-outs from reactive/contaminated sand — what is the mechanism and the prevention for each?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: freeze-thaw spalls saturated brick/mortar; lime popping = late hydration of unslaked/over-burnt CaO/MgO expanding after the joint has hardened; cure = properly slaked/aged lime + clean aggregate; reactive/dirty sand pops out.

**25. Bond/adhesion failure and separation: what actually breaks the mortar-to-unit bond (low water retention, wrong brick suction/IRA, no tooling, retempering past board life, dusty units, air over-entrainment), and how is each one prevented on the wall?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
Leads: bond wants matched IRA + good water retention + tooling at thumbprint-hard on clean units; each failure mode maps to one prevention.

**26. Initial Rate of Absorption (IRA): how does the owner measure a brick's suction, what threshold means "wet the brick before laying," and how do BOTH too-thirsty and too-slick (glazed/low-IRA) units ruin bond and invite joint cracking?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [TEST-TO-PROVE] [METHOD]
Leads: IRA = grams absorbed per minute per 30 in²; units above ~30 g/min pre-wet 3–24 hr; very low-IRA units float/slide and bond poorly the other way; simple pan-of-water field test.

**27. Mortar–brick incompatibility: how does the owner match a new mortar's hardness and permeability to a given brick — especially reclaimed, soft, or owner-made units — and what field cues warn that the mortar has ended up harder than the brick?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: adjust lime:cement within the proportion spec to soften/permeate the joint; the tell is cracks running through brick faces rather than through joints.

**28. Joint tooling: why do concave and V joints out-perform raked/struck/flush/extruded joints for water resistance, exactly when is a joint "thumbprint hard" for tooling, and how does the tooling action densify the surface skin and seal the joint?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: tool at thumbprint-hard; concave/V compact the mortar and shed water; raked/struck/flush leave ledges or fail to compact — discouraged in freezing climates.

**29. Full bed vs face-shell bedding, and full head joints: how do furrowed/partial beds and slushed or unfilled head joints create the leak paths and weak planes that later "separate," and what does proper joint filling look like for veneer versus loadbearing block?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: furrowing the bed and slushing head joints are the classic leak/low-bond shortcuts; full head joints are critical to veneer water resistance.

**30. How does the owner cut out and repoint a failed or cracked joint decades later — cutout depth (about 2–2½× joint width, back to sound mortar), avoiding saw/grinder damage to the units, prep (dampen, remove dust), packing in thin lifts, and re-tooling to match?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: rake to ~2–2½× width to sound mortar; hand tools or careful grinder to avoid widening/burning brick; pack thin compacted lifts; match the profile.

**31. How is a repointing mortar matched to the existing wall (no harder than the units, matched color/sand/permeability), and why is a hard portland patch on a soft old or owner-made wall the single most destructive repair mistake?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: repoint mortar must be as-soft-or-softer and as-permeable-or-more than the original and the units; match sand for color/texture; portland patch spalls soft brick.

**32. What simple field/lab tests can the owner run to prove his mortar and workmanship before committing the wall — flow/cone consistency, a water-retention feel test, a bond-wrench or crossed-brick couplet bond test, cube compressive tests, and above all a full-size mock-up panel?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [TEST-TO-PROVE] [METHOD]
Leads: ASTM C1437 flow, C1506 water retention, C1072 bond wrench / couplet, C109 cubes; build and evaluate a mock-up before the real wall.

**33. What should the owner watch for during the grout pour and the day's laying that early-warns of trouble — fins/voids/lift lines in grout, consistent joint color, joints tooling cleanly at the right moment, cavity staying clean — i.e., a build-day QA checklist?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

**34. What is the real materials cost and coverage math — bags of portland vs lime vs pre-blended mortar/mortar cement, sand by the yard, admixture cost per thousand units, pigment cost — and where does an owner-builder actually save vs waste money across site-mix, bagged, and silo/pre-blend options?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [COST] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]

**35. What tools and consumables does mixing, laying, and pointing actually require (paddle mixer vs tub-and-hoe, boards, jointers sized to the joint, tuckpointing bag/gun, cavity board/deflection net), and what is the minimum viable kit for a solo owner-builder?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [COST] [METHOD]

### From the structure and envelope problems-and-solutions research (draft D)

**36. In grouted reinforced masonry, what causes grout voids, incompletely filled cells, and blowouts, and how do low-lift vs high-lift grouting, lift limits, and consolidation prevent them?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: low-lift built ≤5 ft then grouted; high-lift needs cleanouts and vibration; consolidate lifts >12"; re-consolidate 5-15 min after placement to close shrinkage voids and arching.

**37. Why are cleanouts and inspection openings required at the base of grouted walls, what goes wrong when mortar droppings foul the cells, and how do masons keep cells clean during lay-up?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE]
Leads: high-lift cleanouts spaced ≤32" / at each rebar, formed by omitting a face shell and sealed before grouting; debris blocks grout flow and rebar bond.

**38. What causes efflorescence, how do masons diagnose the moisture source and salt origin (unit vs mortar vs backup), and what prevents recurring staining?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]

**39. Which mortar mistakes cause leaks and weak walls (wrong Type N/S/M for the application, retempering, poor joint tooling, furrowed bed joints), and what mixing/tooling discipline do pro masons follow?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: over-strong mortar cracks the units; concave tooled joints shed water; full head and bed joints for water resistance.

**40. How do cold- and hot-weather conditions damage fresh masonry and grout, and what cold/hot-weather masonry procedures protect the work?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE]

**41. What creative reinforced-masonry approaches can improve a durable owner-built wall (bond-beam layouts, surface-bonding cement for dry-stack CMU, split-face/architectural units, through-body color, prism testing for QC)?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]
Leads: surface-bonding cement lets an owner dry-stack CMU then parge both faces; bond beams for crack/seismic control.

**42. How are lintels and bond beams over openings built in reinforced masonry/concrete (bearing length, reinforcement, avoiding cracking above the opening), and what fails when they are undersized or poorly grouted?**
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

## Envelope, Waterproofing, Thermal Performance, and Radon

### From the structure and envelope problems-and-solutions research (draft D)

**1. How does radon enter through the slab/foundation, and what construction sequence builds an effective passive system that can later be upgraded to active?**
Lands in: Envelope Waterproofing Thermal and Radon. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE]
Leads: gas-permeable layer under slab, continuous 6-mil poly soil-gas retarder, sealed slab penetrations, 3-4" vent stack to roof, roughed-in power for a future fan; Oregon ORSC Appendix AF; mandated counties include Multnomah/Washington/Clackamas/Polk/Yamhill/Hood River/Baker.

**2. What are the most damaging air-barrier discontinuities (top plates, rim/floor bands, penetrations, wall-roof and wall-foundation transitions), and how do builders achieve and verify continuity (blower-door target, red-lined barrier)?**
Lands in: Envelope Waterproofing Thermal and Radon. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: air-tightness primacy; blower-door testing; draw one continuous "red line" around the section — transitions are the weak points.

**3. How do interior humidity plus air leakage produce hidden condensation and mold inside walls and roofs, and what mix of mechanical ventilation (HRV/ERV, spot exhaust), air-sealing, and vapor control keeps assemblies safe?**
Lands in: Envelope Waterproofing Thermal and Radon. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: tighten the house, then ventilate on purpose; mold forms where warm moist interior air reaches cold sheathing.

**4. Which insulation-installation defects quietly waste the biggest share of performance (compressed/voided batts, gaps, wind-washing, thermal bypasses, missing continuous insulation), and how do pros install to Grade-I quality?**
Lands in: Envelope Waterproofing Thermal and Radon. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

**5. Where are the worst thermal bridges in a concrete/masonry home (slab edge, shelf angles/balconies, lintels, ties, wall-floor junctions), and which thermal-break products and details cut them?**
Lands in: Envelope Waterproofing Thermal and Radon. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: slab-edge and sub-slab insulation; thermal-break structural connectors; continuous exterior insulation over mass.

**6. Which water-resistive-barrier and drainage-plane mistakes cause bulk-water intrusion (reverse laps, failing seam tape, no drainage gap behind cladding, incompatible sealants), and what shingle-lap/rain-screen discipline prevents them?**
Lands in: Envelope Waterproofing Thermal and Radon. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

**7. For a durable mass-wall envelope, what creative continuous-insulation and thermal-mass strategies work (interior vs exterior insulation on mass, insulation fully outboard for a "thermal battery," phase-change materials, bermed/earth-coupled walls), and what are the moisture/detailing tradeoffs?**
Lands in: Envelope Waterproofing Thermal and Radon. [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: mass pays off when insulation is outboard so the mass couples to the interior; earth-sheltered/bermed walls demand robust waterproofing and drainage.

**8. For a rural crawlspace or basement, is a vented or sealed/conditioned approach better in the Oregon marine climate, and what moisture failures does each risk (ground vapor, summer humid-air condensing on cool surfaces)?**
Lands in: Envelope Waterproofing Thermal and Radon. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: closed/conditioned crawlspace with a ground vapor barrier is increasingly favored; vented crawls can condense summer humidity on cool surfaces.

**9. What are the most common window/door flashing failures (no sill pan, reverse-lapped head flashing, sealant-only installs, missing back dam), and what pan-flashing sequence keeps openings watertight in a masonry wall?**
Lands in: Envelope Waterproofing Thermal and Radon. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: sill pan with back dam and end dams; head flashing lapped over the WRB, jambs lapped correctly; "sealant is not flashing."

**10. What causes condensation and rot around windows (frame thermal bridging, interior air leakage, poor sill slope), and how do builders detail warm, airtight, well-drained openings?**
Lands in: Envelope Waterproofing Thermal and Radon. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

## Roof System and Roof-Wall Junctions

### From the structure and envelope problems-and-solutions research (draft D)

**1. Where do roof-to-wall junctions leak and fail most (parapet/coping, cavity-wall-to-roof tie-in, missing kick-out flashing, roof WRB not lapped to wall WRB), and what detailing keeps water and air continuous across the joint?**
Lands in: Roof System and Roof-Wall Junctions. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: kick-out flashing at roof-wall intersections prevents wall saturation; through-wall flashing/coping at parapets; maintain continuity of the air and water barriers across the transition.

**2. For a heavy concrete/masonry home, which roof-structure options best resist wind and fire and manage the transition to mass walls (concrete roof deck, SIPs, framed trusses, insulated flat vs pitched), and what condensation risks does each carry?**
Lands in: Roof System and Roof-Wall Junctions. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**3. What causes attic/roof-deck condensation in cold-marine conditions (air leakage into the attic, unbalanced ventilation, unvented assemblies detailed wrong), and how are vented and unvented roofs detailed to stay dry?**
Lands in: Roof System and Roof-Wall Junctions. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: air-seal the ceiling plane first; unvented roofs need enough exterior/foam R to keep the sheathing above dew point; balance ridge and soffit venting.

**4. How are roof overhangs, gutters, and drainage designed to protect masonry walls ("a good hat and boots"), and how much does masonry durability suffer with minimal overhangs?**
Lands in: Roof System and Roof-Wall Junctions. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: generous overhangs cut wall wetting and inward vapor drive; rain-screen cavities pay off most where overhangs are short.

Does this project's roof overhang depth (already a composition and rain-protection question under Entry Composition) also function as the primary moss/algae prevention measure for the wall below it, and can overhang depth be sized with both purposes stated as one requirement rather than two separate ones?**

What real, built examples exist of a Pacific Northwest concrete-and-masonry house (not a commercial or institutional building) that combines exposed concrete or masonry with wood and steel in this exact climate, beyond the single named lead (Tom Kundig/Olson Kundig) found in this research pass?**

Are there other vernacular masonry or concrete traditions, beyond the three found in this research pass (Ando/Japan, Barragán/Mexico, Baker/India), that specifically address a wet, temperate, marine climate similar to western Oregon, e.g., traditions from the UK, coastal Scandinavia, or coastal Chile?**

File: `_MERGED_3_systems.md`

Merged Wave-3 research question bank for: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water; Water Independence; Plumbing: DWV, Sewage, Venting, Slope, Distance, and Fixtures; Electrical: Service, Panels, Grounding, and Load Planning; Electrical: Branch Circuits, Wire Size, Voltage Drop, Lighting, Low Voltage, and Future Power; Solar, Battery, and Energy Independence; HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality; Smart Home, Network, Security, and Local Automation; Interiors, Finishes, Accessibility, and Livability. Consolidated from drafts E, F, H, I, J, K, L, M, N, Q, S, T and the main research-questions file's Section B seed items. No question here has been answered; this is an organizing pass only.

---

## Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water

**1.** Pressure-tank short-cycling and waterlogging, the bladder-failure story. What actually causes a well pressure tank to short-cycle (waterlogged/ruptured bladder, wrong air precharge, oversized pump), how to size drawdown and set precharge for a chosen cut-in/cut-out (e.g. 30/50 vs 40/60), and why iron/manganese sediment and chlorine shorten bladder life. Include the field diagnosis and the owner-replaceable fix.
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [COST]
Leads: midatlanticwater / freshwatersystems: Schrader-valve spray test confirms ruptured bladder; drain-and-recharge only buys 3-12 months; iron/Cl embrittles the bladder; precharge should sit ~2 psi below cut-in.

**2.** Constant-pressure / variable-speed (VFD) pumping as the creative alternative. For steady whole-house pressure and far fewer cycles, when does a soft-start VFD constant-pressure submersible beat a conventional tank? Weigh pump-life/comfort gains against the off-grid downsides (continuous electronics draw, surge/lightning vulnerability, higher cost, harder rural repair).
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [COST]

**3.** Well-water treatment train, getting the order right. For the common rural-Oregon cocktail (iron, manganese, iron bacteria, hardness, low pH, tannins, occasional coliform), what is the correct sequence of stages and why does order control success (sediment, then oxidation/iron filter, then softener, then carbon, then UV; UV always last; softener before any RO)? What does a water test have to measure before you can design it?
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [METHOD] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: SpringWell / worldwaterreserve: UV must see already-clear water to work; iron/hardness must come out upstream or they foul the softener and shadow the UV lamp.

**4.** Iron/sulfur bacteria and biofilm, the recurring slime-and-smell problem. Why shock chlorination keeps failing to hold, and what a durable solution looks like: continuous chlorine or hydrogen-peroxide injection with a contact/retention tank ahead of the pressure tank, plus filtration. Are there off-grid-friendlier (low-power, non-chemical) routes?
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: worldwaterreserve / SpringWell: shock kills but bacteria recolonize; H2O2 or Cl injection plus retention tank plus iron filter plus UV is the "solved" pattern people land on.

**5.** Aggressive (low-pH, soft) water eating copper, pinhole leaks and blue-green stains. Diagnose acidic well or rainwater, compare the calcite/acid-neutralizer tank vs soda-ash injection, and confirm this is the argument for running the whole house in PEX rather than copper.
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD]
Leads: rainwater sources (WRAP / NTO) flag low-pH rain corroding thin copper; same neutralizer fix applies to soft well water; PEX sidesteps it entirely.

**6.** Whole-house pressure design: PRV, thermal expansion, and water hammer. Setting incoming pressure, when a closed system (check valve / PRV / backflow) forces a thermal-expansion tank on the water heater, and where water-hammer arrestors belong (washer, dishwasher, fast-closing solenoid valves).
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [CODE] [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**7.** Freeze protection for the well head, pressure tank, and exposed lines. Pitless-adapter/burial depth below frost, insulated pump house vs heat-tape vs self-draining layout, and how off-grid changes the math (heat tape is a nasty winter phantom load on the battery).
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]

**8.** Home-run (manifold) PEX vs trunk-and-branch. A solo builder's case for a central labeled manifold: individual shutoffs, no buried in-wall fittings, easy troubleshooting, versus more pipe volume, longer wait-for-hot on long runs, and cost. When is each right?
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD] [COST]

**9.** Heat-pump water heater placement, it cools and dries the room it stands in. Where does an HPWH go in an all-electric concrete house so it isn't strangled by a cold winter garage? Cover ducting options, condensate routing, noise, minimum room volume, the split (compressor-outside) variant, and turning its cool dry exhaust into summer dehumidification.
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]
Leads: ENERGY STAR / attainablehome: drops to resistance below ~37-40F; wants ~700-1000 cubic feet or ducting; split HPWH puts the noisy/cooling compressor outdoors, the cold-climate creative fix.

**10.** Sizing an HPWH and soaking midday solar, the "hot-water battery." First-hour rating vs recovery for the household, and running the tank on a timer/relay to consume midday PV so hot water becomes cheap stored energy.
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD] [COST]

**11.** Long hot-water waits and wasted water in a spread-out house. Demand-controlled recirculation (button/motion-triggered, not a continuous loop that bleeds standby heat), structured-plumbing pipe sizing, and point-of-use boosters for far fixtures.
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]

**12.** One heat source for both domestic hot water and radiant floors, combi vs separate. Legionella and scald/mixing-valve issues when storage runs low for radiant, buffer-tank strategy, and the open-loop (potable water through the floor) vs closed-loop code limits.
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water / HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE] [METHOD]

**13.** Well siting and yield, the independence question at the source. Interpreting a drawdown/yield test, OWRD exempt-groundwater limits, required separations from septic/property lines/contamination sources, and the low-yield-well fix (large storage cistern plus booster pump buffering a trickle well).
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water (cross-ref Land, Site, and Feasibility)
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: OWRD exempt-use groundwater framework; low-yield homesteads commonly solve gpm shortfalls with a cistern buffer feeding a pressure pump.

**14.** What size dedicated wildfire-suppression water reserve (gallons) and what delivery system (dry hydrant to a pond, a dedicated exterior standpipe fed from the domestic cistern, a pressurized wildfire sprinkler kit with its own pump) makes sense for this specific rural Oregon property, sized against NFPA 1142's method rather than a generic minimum, and how is that reserve combined with, not duplicated against, the domestic deep-water-store sizing?
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water (cross-ref Resilience and Disaster Independence)
Tags: [METHOD] [COST] [CODE]

**15.** What combined cistern-plus-well-plus-rainwater-harvest sizing gives this specific household a stated number of autonomy days (no grid, no functioning well pump, no incoming municipal or hauled water) at both routine-use and conservation-mode consumption rates, and where is the point of diminishing return on cistern size against cost and footprint?
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water (cross-ref Resilience and Disaster Independence)
Tags: [METHOD] [COST]

**16.** What gravity-fed and non-electric water filtration products (Berkey-class, LifeStraw-class, or larger whole-household gravity systems) exist at a scale appropriate for an extended, not just short-trip, household emergency, what is their rated throughput and filter-replacement interval, and how do they compare on total cost of ownership against a UV or RO system that requires power?
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water (cross-ref Resilience and Disaster Independence)
Tags: [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [COST] [METHOD]

**17.** What does a documented shock-chlorination procedure specific to this project's well construction (casing depth, pitless adapter, pump type already specified elsewhere) look like step by step, and at what point does the extension-service guidance to hire a licensed well driller rather than self-perform apply?
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water (cross-ref Resilience and Disaster Independence)
Tags: [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [COST]

**18.** What manual (non-electric) backup water-access method (a hand pump installed alongside the electric well pump, a bucket-and-pulley system, a gravity-fed cistern tap) is realistic to install at this project's specific well depth and pump type, and what does it cost against the value of the water access it guarantees when both grid and generator fuel are exhausted?
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD] [COST]

**19.** How is the combined fire-suppression-and-domestic cistern reserve plumbed so the fire-suppression draw cannot accidentally leave the household without domestic water, and does Oregon or the local fire jurisdiction have a required minimum reserved (non-domestic-accessible) fire volume?
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water (cross-ref Resilience and Disaster Independence)
Tags: [METHOD] [CODE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**20.** What water storage container/tank material (concrete, welded steel, bolted steel, poly/HDPE, fiberglass) performs best for a buried, multi-decade, freeze-safe cistern serving this project's long-life philosophy, and does an owner-poured concrete cistern (consistent with the project's blockmaking/alternative-concrete research track) compete on cost and durability against a manufactured tank?
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water (cross-ref Resilience and Disaster Independence)
Tags: [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [COST] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

**21.** What is the realistic year-round rainwater harvest volume for this project's actual roof area and the specific parcel's rainfall pattern (accounting for the dry summer months when demand peaks and supply is lowest), and does the harvest calculation change the cistern sizing conclusion?
Lands in: Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water (cross-ref Resilience and Disaster Independence)
Tags: [METHOD] [COST]

**22.** Is a terminal chlorine residual (as used at the Bullitt Center to protect water sitting in distribution piping) necessary or advisable for a single-family system with much shorter pipe runs and faster turnover, or does UV alone suffice if the system is designed to avoid long standing-water dead legs, consistent with the dead-leg rule already established in this chapter?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**23.** For a rainwater train feeding the same house as a well train, is a single shared point-of-entry disinfection and pressure system workable (both sources treated and blended before one distribution system), or does keeping the two sources and trains fully separate until the fixture level reduce cross-contamination and troubleshooting risk enough to justify the extra piping and equipment?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

**24.** What does Oregon's Appendix K potable-rainwater-catchment pathway (already named in this chapter as rare for single-family use, requiring Oregon Health Authority involvement) actually require in practice, has any Oregon single-family project completed it, and is Appendix K's process realistically more or less burdensome than running the rainwater system as a thoroughly treated nonpotable system that never legally claims potable status?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [CODE] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]
Leads: 2023 OPSC Appendix K; OHA involvement requirement already noted in the existing water-supply chapter text, unverified for any completed Oregon single-family precedent.

**25.** What is the actual annual operating cost and failure history (lamp burnout, ballast failure, sleeve fouling) of a residential UV disinfection unit run on well water plus intermittent rainwater of varying turbidity, from real owner reports rather than a manufacturer's rated lamp life?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [COST] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**26.** Does a whole-house carbon stage sized for a well's chlorine/chloramine and taste/odor profile need a different media or cartridge change interval than the same stage sized for rainwater's organic/tannin load, if the two sources ever share downstream carbon filtration?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD]

**27.** For a well testing positive for elevated radon, does packed-tower aeration or GAC adsorption fit better into this project's off-grid resilience goals (aeration needs continuous fan power; GAC needs periodic media replacement and creates a low-level radioactive-waste handling question), and what does each cost installed and to operate?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [METHOD] [COST] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**28.** What is the actual protocol (chlorine concentration, contact time, mechanical scrubbing scope) for clearing an iron- or sulfur-bacteria-fouled well, reconciling the two different shock-concentration figures found in research (roughly 200 ppm general shock versus roughly 500 ppm specifically for iron/sulfur bacteria), and at what point does a persistent case require a well-service contractor rather than an owner-performed shock treatment?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [COST]

**29.** How does the well-water treatment train's stage order (already fully specified in this chapter) actually change, if at all, when the source is a low-yield well buffered through a storage tank rather than a direct-pressurized high-yield well, given that stored raw water ahead of treatment can itself grow biofilm or lose chlorine residual while it sits?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**30.** What water-quality changes, if any, does a hydrofractured or chemically redeveloped well show in the months after treatment (turbidity spike, temporary bacteriological positive, mineral content shift), and does the existing post-construction bacteria re-sampling schedule (first heavy rain, three months in) need to be repeated after any major well rehabilitation event?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [TEST-TO-PROVE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**31.** Where this house runs both a well supply and a rainwater supply, what is the actual measured pH, hardness, and alkalinity difference between the two sources likely to be for this specific site and roof material, and does that difference require two separately corrected distribution zones rather than one shared point-of-entry correction?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [TEST-TO-PROVE] [METHOD]

**32.** For any galvanized, copper, or other metallic fitting downstream of a rainwater train, does intermittent or seasonal flow (a rainwater system that may sit unused for stretches during the wet season if the well is primary) change the corrosion behavior compared to the continuous-flow assumption the existing corrosion-control material is written around?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

**33.** Does OPSC apply a hazard-tiered backflow-device logic similar to the Australian AS/NZS 3500.1 pattern (an aboveground tank rated lower-hazard, satisfied by a simpler device; a buried tank rated higher-hazard, requiring a testable reduced-pressure assembly), or does Oregon's code require the same device class regardless of tank placement, and which reading governs this project's actual buried-versus-aboveground tank decision?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [CODE]

**34.** What automatic source-switching hardware (a WISY-class float-switch-and-solenoid controller, or a functional equivalent available through a US plumbing-supply channel rather than importing European hardware) is realistically buyable and installable in Oregon, and what does a complete rainwater-priority, well-backup switching system cost installed?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [TOOLS] [COST]

**35.** For a rainwater-priority, well-backup switching system, what is the actual failure mode if the controller or its solenoid valve fails (stuck open cross-connecting the two sources, or stuck closed leaving the house without water), and what manual override does the design need so a controller failure is a service call rather than a contamination event or a dry house?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

**36.** Is a fully separate, never-cross-connected plumbing system (rainwater serving only specific fixtures or zones, well serving the rest, with the household manually choosing which tap to use) a realistic and simpler alternative to an automatic blending/switching system, and what does that do to fixture count, pipe runs, and labeling requirements already established for this chapter?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]

**37.** What testing and certification burden does a testable backflow assembly at the well/rainwater interface add on an ongoing basis (the annual certified-tester requirement already established generically for this chapter), and is there a rural Oregon certified backflow tester within a reasonable drive of this project's likely site, or does that drive time itself become a real maintenance cost?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [COST] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

**38.** Does combining a well and a rainwater system change the water-heater and hot-water-delivery design already fully specified in this chapter (for example, does an intermittently used rainwater line feeding a fixture need its own freeze and stagnation protection separate from the always-flowing well line), or does the existing hot-water design apply unchanged regardless of which source feeds it?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

**40.** Is hydrofracturing worth attempting on a marginal Oregon bedrock well before committing to a full storage-and-booster buffering system, given the reported wide variance in outcome, and what does a hydrofracturing attempt cost against the cost of simply building the buffer system regardless of outcome?
Lands in: Water Independence / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [COST] [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**41.** What real Oregon well-driller experience exists with hydrofracturing, airlift redevelopment, and chemical/mechanical rehabilitation specifically in the state's common bedrock types (basalt, other volcanic formations), as opposed to the granite and sedimentary bedrock most hydrofracturing case studies are drawn from?

Tags: [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [METHOD]

Tags: [TOOLS] [METHOD]

## Water Independence

**1.** For each roof material this project is actually considering (standing-seam metal, bare or coated flat concrete deck, membrane roofing), what is the verified runoff coefficient and the verified water-quality profile (pH, metals, organic load) from a primary source rather than a secondary aggregator?
Lands in: Water Independence
Tags: [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD]
Leads: American Rainwater Catchment Systems Association roofing guidance; comparative roofing-material rainwater-quality studies (ScienceDirect, several regional studies) as a starting bibliography, unverified.

**2.** How does a bare concrete roof's runoff pH and alkalinity change over the first several years as the surface carbonates, and does a concrete sealer or coating change that trajectory in a way that matters for downstream treatment design?
Lands in: Water Independence (cross-ref Envelope, Waterproofing, Thermal Performance, and Radon)
Tags: [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
Leads: general concrete-carbonation literature; roofing-material rainwater-quality comparative studies, unverified for the specific carbonation-over-time question.

**3.** What actual short-duration, high-return-period rainfall intensity (5-minute or 15-minute, 25- or 100-year) applies to this project's specific parcel, from a direct NOAA Atlas 14 pull, and how does gutter and downspout sizing change once that figure replaces a generic regional estimate?
Lands in: Water Independence
Tags: [METHOD] [TOOLS]
Leads: NOAA Atlas 14 point-precipitation-frequency tool, unverified for the specific parcel.

**4.** What gutter and downspout material, profile, and hanger spacing hold up best under Oregon's combination of high-volume winter rain, needle/leaf debris from conifers, occasional heavy wet snow load, and UV exposure, and which materials are safe for a potable-intent catchment train versus non-potable-only?
Lands in: Water Independence
Tags: [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**5.** What screening options (basket strainer, leaf-eater, vortex/centrifugal filter) are available at the residential scale, what do they cost installed, and how much sediment load do they actually remove ahead of the first-flush diverter, backed by a manufacturer test result rather than a marketing claim?
Lands in: Water Independence
Tags: [TOOLS] [COST] [TEST-TO-PROVE]
Leads: WISY and similar European rainwater-hardware manufacturers publish vortex-filter flow and removal-rate data, unverified.

**6.** For a conifer-heavy rural Oregon lot, what gutter-guard or screen maintenance schedule actually prevents needle-mat buildup across a wet season, and what does skipping it cost in first-flush volume and filter-clogging downstream?
Lands in: Water Independence
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

**7.** Can the same roof surface feed both a fire-suppression cistern and a domestic-supply cistern from one catchment system, or does the fire-reserve volume need a physically separate catchment and conveyance path to avoid a single point of failure taking out both?

Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

Tags: [METHOD] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

## Plumbing: DWV, Sewage, Venting, Slope, Distance, and Fixtures

**1.** Septic drainfield premature failure, the biomat / solids-carryover problem. The dominant real-world failure chain (skipped pumping leads to solids/grease reaching the field leads to biomat clogging the soil) and the prevention stack: effluent filter and its cleaning, pump interval, keeping vehicles/roots/compaction off the field, and what never goes down the drain.
Lands in: Plumbing: DWV, Sewage, Venting, Slope, Distance, and Fixtures
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [CODE]
Leads: NCSU Extension / WA-DOH / inspectapedia: the number-one cause is not pumping on schedule; the effluent filter (required on post-1999 permits) must be cleaned at pump-out or it chokes flow.

**2.** Septic type selection on a hard site (Oregon DEQ terms). For high water table / shallow or slow-perc soil, walk the ladder: conventional gravity vs pressure-distribution vs sand filter vs ATU vs mound/capping fill, with siting, required reserve/replacement area, and the cost climb. What does the site/perc evaluation decide?
Lands in: Plumbing: DWV, Sewage, Venting, Slope, Distance, and Fixtures (cross-ref Land, Site, and Feasibility)
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [COST] [CODE]
Leads: Oregon DEQ onsite/subsurface wastewater program: soil and perc evaluation gates the whole feasibility question; reserve area is mandatory.

**3.** Creative wastewater load-cutters, graywater reuse and composting toilets, what Oregon allows. Oregon's graywater reuse permit tiers, whether an indoor composting toilet is accepted, and how each reduces septic load and extends field life on a marginal site.
Lands in: Plumbing: DWV, Sewage, Venting, Slope, Distance, and Fixtures
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [CODE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: Oregon DEQ graywater reuse permit program exists (roof/interior sources by tier); verify current rules and whether composting toilets change the required septic sizing.

**4.** DWV venting you can't redo later in a masonry/concrete house. Wet venting, island/loop vents for a kitchen island, air-admittance valves where OPSC permits them, and a slab-embedded drain layout that has to be right the first time.
Lands in: Plumbing: DWV, Sewage, Venting, Slope, Distance, and Fixtures
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [CODE]

**5.** Under-slab DWV, get it perfect before the pour. Trench and slope layout, sleeving through the slab, pressure/test-ball testing the buried system before concrete, protecting stub-ups, and sealing every slab penetration for both radon and water.
Lands in: Plumbing: DWV, Sewage, Venting, Slope, Distance, and Fixtures (cross-ref Build Sequence and Site Coordination)
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [TOOLS]

## Electrical: Service, Panels, Grounding, and Load Planning

**1.** Concrete-encased electrode (Ufer), the vapor-barrier trap. Why rebar in a slab poured over poly cannot count as the CEE (broken earth contact), how to use the footing instead, the exact stub-up/connection detail cast before the pour, and the fallback when the whole foundation sits on foam/vapor barrier.
Lands in: Electrical: Service, Panels, Grounding, and Load Planning
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE] [METHOD]
Leads: ExpertCE / Texas-inspector: needs at least 20 ft of half-inch rebar or bare #4 Cu, at least 2 inches cover, in direct earth contact; slab-on-poly disqualifies the slab, so the CEE goes in the footing and must be planned pre-pour.

**2.** Grounding, bonding, and surge protection for a steel-and-electronics house. Foundation electrode plus supplemental electrodes, bonding of any gas/CSST and metallic water, DC grounding for the solar/battery, and whole-house SPD at the panel to protect the well pump and electronics from rural lightning and utility surges.
Lands in: Electrical: Service, Panels, Grounding, and Load Planning
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]

**3.** Service and panel sizing for an all-electric load. Running an NEC-220 load calc for heat pump plus HPWH plus induction range plus EV plus well pump, why 200 A often ends up tight, and the fixes: load-management/EVEMS to dodge a service upgrade, or a 320 A meter-main. What margin should a long-life house design in?
Lands in: Electrical: Service, Panels, Grounding, and Load Planning
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [CODE] [COST]

**4.** Where solar/battery ties in, main vs sub-panel and the busbar rule. Line-side (supply-side) tap vs load-side breaker, the 120 percent busbar interconnection limit, whole-home backup vs a protected/critical-loads sub-panel, and provisioning spare panel space now.
Lands in: Electrical: Service, Panels, Grounding, and Load Planning / Solar, Battery, and Energy Independence
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

**5.** Backup interconnection safety, transfer switch, interlock, and the backfeed hazard. Manual transfer switch vs listed interlock kit vs whole-home ATS, why a "suicide cord" kills, and how a battery-inverter with a microgrid interconnect device changes the whole wiring picture.
Lands in: Electrical: Service, Panels, Grounding, and Load Planning / Solar, Battery, and Energy Independence
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE] [METHOD]

**6.** What does the NEC 702.5 interlock-kit-versus-dedicated-transfer-switch decision cost and require for this project's specific panel, and does the local Oregon AHJ accept an interlock kit or require a dedicated transfer switch regardless of NEC permissibility?
Lands in: Electrical: Service, Panels, Grounding, and Load Planning / Solar, Battery, and Energy Independence
Tags: [CODE] [COST]

**7.** Energized electrical work: state precisely where the code-mandated two-person rule applies (above 600 V) and where it does not (ordinary residential branch circuits and panels), and separately state the practice-recommendation case for never doing residential panel work alone even though it is not code-mandated. Avoid implying a code requirement that does not exist at residential voltage.
Lands in: Electrical: Service, Panels, Grounding, and Load Planning (cross-ref The Owner-Builder's Body and Mind)
Tags: [CODE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: OSHA mandates two people only above 600 V (utility-class); professional practice literature recommends never working alone on any energized panel regardless of voltage, reasoning that a second person can cut power or start CPR immediately.

## Electrical: Branch Circuits, Wire Size, Voltage Drop, Lighting, Low Voltage, and Future Power

**1.** Conduit and box provisioning in poured/masonry walls, the "add a circuit later" is brutal here. Chases and embedded conduit runs, extra home runs, empty spare conduits to key locations, and the veteran concrete-home "wish I'd run one more" list captured before concrete goes up.

Lands in: Electrical: Branch Circuits, Wire Size, Voltage Drop, Lighting, Low Voltage, and Future Power
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [CODE]

Lands in: Electrical: Branch Circuits, Wire Size, Voltage Drop, Lighting, Low Voltage, and Future Power
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [COST] [METHOD]

**5.** Lighting without an electrician-later regret, neutrals, dimming, and DC. Why smart switches need a neutral at every box, dimmer/LED compatibility, and the synergy of low-voltage/DC lighting in a battery house (no inverter losses, native dimming).
Lands in: Electrical: Branch Circuits, Wire Size, Voltage Drop, Lighting, Low Voltage, and Future Power / Smart Home, Network, Security, and Local Automation

Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

## HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality

**1.** One heat source for both domestic hot water and radiant floors, combi vs separate. Legionella and scald/mixing-valve issues when storage runs low for radiant, buffer-tank strategy, and the open-loop (potable water through the floor) vs closed-loop code limits.
Lands in: HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality / Plumbing: Water Supply, Pressure, Treatment, and Hot Water
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE] [METHOD]

**2.** Radiant-slab thermal lag and overheating in a superinsulated concrete house. Why a high-mass slab overshoots on a sunny afternoon (slow response plus passive-solar gain), and the control answer: outdoor-reset, slab sensors, anticipatory/weather-compensated logic, plus the low-mass (thin-slab / staple-up) alternative for responsiveness.
Lands in: HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: Concrete Network / GBA / Barron Heating: high-mass slabs overheat with any extra gain unless outdoor-reset plus anticipatory control; a very well-insulated house may over-deliver from the slab lag alone.

**3.** Air-to-water heat pump driving the radiant floor, cold-climate reality. Buffer-tank sizing to stop short-cycling, glycol vs water and freeze protection, defrost behavior on a hydronic system, and which cold-climate air-to-water units actually exist for this house.
Lands in: HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]

**4.** Defrost cycles and backup-heat behavior on an air-source heat pump. The 25-35F frost zone, how resistance strips fire during defrost (and can spike an off-grid load), and why radiant thermal mass rides through a defrost dip better than forced air.
Lands in: HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]
Leads: Lennox / GBA: frost is worst 25-35F, defrost checks every 30-90 minutes and lasts 5-15 minutes; strips energize during defrost; slab mass buffers the temporary loss.

**5.** Does a superinsulated concrete box even need radiant? Steelman the "one or two minisplits heat the whole house" argument against radiant comfort, the hybrid middle path (small radiant zone plus ductless), and an honest cost/complexity comparison for an owner-builder.
Lands in: HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [COST]

**6.** Cooling and dehumidification in a radiant-heated house, radiant can't cool safely. Adding ductless/ducted heat-pump cooling, the condensation risk if you try to chill a slab below dew point, and whether a standalone whole-house dehumidifier is needed in humid PNW shoulder seasons.
Lands in: HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

**7.** Zoning a high-thermal-mass house, when zoning fights the mass. Why aggressive multi-zone control struggles against a slab that changes slowly, a realistic zone count, actuator/manifold reliability, and near-single-setpoint steady-state operation as the low-tech creative answer.
Lands in: HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

**8.** HRV vs ERV for a tight house in marine/mixed Oregon. The moisture-balance argument (ERV smooths winter dryness and summer humidity), core-frost control in cold weather, and the condensate drain/trap requirement. Which is the defensible default here and why?
Lands in: HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: GBA / Energy Vanguard: "you probably want an ERV"; PNW air feels damp but is drier than indoor air in absolute terms; frost defrost on the ERV core is the counter-consideration.

**9.** Balanced-ventilation ducting and commissioning, the part people skip. Dedicated HRV/ERV ducting vs sharing the heat-pump air handler, balancing supply and exhaust airflow, filter (MERV) pressure-drop penalty, and actually measuring delivered CFM vs nameplate.
Lands in: HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality

## Smart Home, Network, Security, and Local Automation

**1.** Local-first vs cloud, surviving vendor sunset and internet outages. Choosing Zigbee / Z-Wave / Matter-over-Thread on a local hub (e.g. Home Assistant) so devices keep working when a company bricks its cloud or paywalls its API; the "you leased it, you didn't own it" failure mode and how to avoid e-waste.
Lands in: Smart Home, Network, Security, and Local Automation
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]
Leads: XDA / Vucense: cloud-dependent devices die on backend shutdown; a local hub keeps automation on-site with sub-10ms latency and full function during outages.

**2.** RF goes to die in rebar and masonry, mesh reliability in a concrete house. How concrete/CMU/rebar attenuate 2.4 GHz Zigbee/Thread and Z-Wave, and the design answer: a wired backbone, repeaters/routers placed with intent, and conduit/access points planned during the pour.
Lands in: Smart Home, Network, Security, and Local Automation
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: reinforced concrete heavily attenuates 2.4 GHz mesh; practitioners lean on wired Ethernet backbones plus mains-powered repeaters and pre-planned in-wall conduit.

**3.** Smart-home security and network segmentation. An isolated IoT VLAN, keeping cameras and logic local, firmware/update discipline, and why an independence-minded owner avoids always-listening cloud microphones.
Lands in: Smart Home, Network, Security, and Local Automation
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

**4.** Monitoring as preventive maintenance. Leak sensors with an automatic whole-house shutoff valve, well/cistern level, freeze alarms in the pump house, battery/inverter telemetry, and a septic high-level alarm, the sensor set that turns disasters into notifications.
Lands in: Smart Home, Network, Security, and Local Automation
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

**5.** Structured cabling you cannot add later. Cat6/fiber home runs, PoE for cameras/APs/sensors, a real network rack/closet, and empty conduit to key rooms, the attic, and the roof (solar, antenna) before walls close.
Lands in: Smart Home, Network, Security, and Local Automation / Electrical: Branch Circuits, Wire Size, Voltage Drop, Lighting, Low Voltage, and Future Power
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD] [TOOLS]

**6.** Graceful degradation, the house must still work when the "brains" are down. Backing the hub/network/well controls on the battery/UPS, and designing physical manual overrides (real switches, manual valves) so a dead server never means a dead house.
Lands in: Smart Home, Network, Security, and Local Automation / Solar, Battery, and Energy Independence
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD]

**7.** Is ShakeAlert earthquake early-warning data available for integration into a residential smart-home/automation system in Oregon today (licensing, latency, false-alarm rate, and what a home automation layer could do with a several-second warning: close the seismic gas valve, release the exterior door locks, alert occupants)?
Lands in: Smart Home, Network, Security, and Local Automation (cross-ref Resilience and Disaster Independence)
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [METHOD] [CODE]
Leads: USGS ShakeAlert Alert Delivery Partner program exists; consumer/single-residence integration appears to be an emerging, not yet standardized, product category.

## Interiors, Finishes, Accessibility, and Livability

**1.** Flooring over a slab, moisture-vapor-emission failures. Why glue-down and rubber-backed floors blister and delaminate over concrete, the RH-probe / MVER testing that must precede installation, the under-slab vapor retarder as the real prevention, and topical moisture-mitigation coatings as the retrofit.
Lands in: Interiors, Finishes, Accessibility, and Livability
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD]
Leads: CTLGroup / Xtreme Polishing: slab RH above roughly 80 percent or MVER above roughly 3 lb/1000 sq ft/24h delaminates adhesives/coatings; rubber backing re-emulsifies the glue; test before you install.

**2.** Polished/stained concrete as the finished floor, the mass-friendly creative option. Crack control (saw-cut joint timing/spacing), stain/dye and sealing/maintenance, comfort and hardness underfoot, and coordinating grind/polish depth with embedded radiant tubing.
Lands in: Interiors, Finishes, Accessibility, and Livability
Tags: [CREATIVE-IDEA] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [METHOD] [COST]

**3.** Curbless / wet-room showers with a slab, waterproofing failure points. Why tile and grout are not the waterproofing, pre-slope plus bonded/sheet membrane vs surface tile, single-plane linear-drain slope, the bonding-flange drain connection, and recessing the slab for a flush entry, decided before the pour.
Lands in: Interiors, Finishes, Accessibility, and Livability / Plumbing: DWV, Sewage, Venting, Slope, Distance, and Fixtures
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: Austin Tile Pro / craftingwithconcrete: leaks come from un-detailed seams/corners/drain and missing pre-slope; quarter-inch-per-foot minimum slope, membrane with a bonding-flange drain, slab recessed pre-pour.

**4.** Radon-resistant new construction, build the passive stack now, add a fan only if a test says so. Sub-slab clean aggregate plus gas-tight membrane plus a 3-inch-plus vent riser cast into the slab, sealed penetrations, Oregon's high-radon-area guidance, and the test-after-occupancy-then-activate logic.
Lands in: Interiors, Finishes, Accessibility, and Livability (cross-ref Build Sequence and Site Coordination)
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CODE] [METHOD] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]
Leads: Oregon OHA Appendix F / W.R. Meadows: 4-inch clean aggregate, at least 3-inch ABS/PVC riser teed into the sub-slab, all penetrations sealed; passive first, wired/roughed so a fan can be added.

**5.** Low-VOC / healthy materials for a tight envelope. Off-gassing risk in a low-air-change house, choosing paints/adhesives/sealants/cabinetry (formaldehyde) accordingly, flush-out/bake-out procedures, and materials that age gracefully over a long-life build.
Lands in: Interiors, Finishes, Accessibility, and Livability / HVAC, Ventilation, Humidity, and Air Quality
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

Lands in: Interiors, Finishes, Accessibility, and Livability (cross-ref The Wall System)
Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

Tags: [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]

File: `_MERGED_4_build_prove_own.md`

Merged Wave 3 question bank for the Part III "Build, Prove, and Own" chapters: Developing the Site: Shop, Block-Yard, and Outbuildings; Tools, Equipment, and the Owner-Builder Shop; Materials, Suppliers, Procurement, and Substitution; Build Sequence and Site Coordination; The Owner-Builder's Body and Mind; Inspection and Approval Sequence; Testing, Commissioning, and Quality Control; Personal Project Finance: Budget, Financing, and Cash Flow; Resilience and Disaster Independence; Documentation, Maintenance, and Future Upgrades; Business and Brand Framework.

Consolidated from drafts A, B, E, F, H, I, J, K, L, M, N, O, P, Q, R, T, U (the seed items in Section C of `research_questions_revision7.md` are folded into Personal Project Finance). Questions only; nothing here is answered. Cross-chapter duplicates are kept where a question genuinely serves two of these chapters.

---

## Developing the Site: Shop, Block-Yard, and Outbuildings

### Site Development Overview and Phasing

1. Does Oregon or the relevant rural county allow an accessory structure (a shop or garage) to be built and occupied before a primary dwelling exists on the parcel, and does living in it before its own certificate of occupancy create a code or insurance problem?
Lands in: Legal, Permits, and Owner-Builder Rules / Developing the Site. [CODE] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: multiple owner-builder forum threads describe "build the shop first, live in it" as common practice nationally, with at least one flag that many jurisdictions technically require the primary residence first; unverified for Oregon specifically.

2. What is the actual ORSC section and current text for the non-habitable detached accessory-structure permit exemption (200 sq ft general, 400 sq ft on a 2-plus-acre parcel with a 20-foot setback), and does it change under the anticipated 2026 ORSC adoption?
Lands in: Legal, Permits, and Owner-Builder Rules / Land, Site, and Feasibility. [CODE]
Leads: general-source figures found (200 sq ft / 15 ft height; 400 sq ft on 2+ acres with 20 ft setback); exact current ORSC section number and the 2026-edition status are unverified.

3. In what order should a solo owner-builder build driveway, temporary utilities, shop, block-yard, and house shell, given this project's specific site, and what is lost or gained by compressing that sequence (for example, running the block-yard concurrently with foundation work instead of finishing it first)?
Lands in: Developing the Site / Build Sequence and Site Coordination. [METHOD] [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

4. What does a realistic laydown and staging plan look like for a rural owner-builder site across the whole build, not just one phase: where do cement, aggregate, block, rebar, lumber, and mechanical/electrical rough-in materials each sit at each stage so nothing blocks truck access or gets buried under the wrong pile?
Lands in: Developing the Site / Build Sequence and Site Coordination. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

5. How far in advance, and to which future outbuilding locations, should spare utility conduit and sleeves be run while the main house trench is open, and what does an owner regret not running once an outbuilding is finally sited years later?
Lands in: Developing the Site / Electrical: Branch Circuits, Wire Size, Voltage Drop, Lighting, Low Voltage, and Future Power. [CREATIVE-IDEA] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

6. How should the site plan resolve conflicts when the septic reserve area, the well setback, the solar array's shade-free envelope, and a desired outbuilding location overlap, and what is the right order to lock each of those onto the site plan first?
Lands in: Land, Site, and Feasibility / Developing the Site. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

7. What real quantitative shade-analysis method (a solar pathfinder reading, a sun-path diagram, or software) should this project run at the site-planning stage to guarantee no future outbuilding, tree, or fence shades the solar array at its mature height, and how far out (in years and in feet) should "future" be modeled?
Lands in: Solar, Battery, and Energy Independence / Developing the Site. [METHOD] [TOOLS] [TEST-TO-PROVE]

8. Are there real cost or schedule savings, beyond the previously researched machine-versus-hand-tool question, from running the block-yard production campaign concurrently with foundation excavation and other early sitework rather than sequentially, and what coordination problems (mud, truck conflicts, dust) does that create?
Lands in: Developing the Site / Build Sequence and Site Coordination. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

### The Owner's Shop

9. What shop floor area, door count, door size, and bay layout supports this project's specific combination of uses (block-making support, tool and material storage, fabrication/welding, vehicle/equipment storage) without oversizing, and how do real owner-builders who also made their own block size their shop in practice?
Lands in: Developing the Site. [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE] [METHOD] [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE]

10. Pole-barn kit versus stick-built versus steel-frame versus shipping-container shop: for a roughly 1,200-2,400 square foot owner-built shop in rural Oregon, what is the real all-in cost (kit or materials, slab, doors, electrical, insulation) for each path in 2026 dollars, and what does each trade off in build time, DIY-feasibility, and long-term durability?
Lands in: Developing the Site / Materials, Suppliers, Procurement, and Substitution. [COST] [OWNER-DESIGN-CHOICE] [MATERIALS/INGREDIENTS]

11. Does a concrete shop floor need to be heated to avoid the condensation problem reported in unheated-slab forums, or do perimeter slab insulation and door-management discipline alone solve it in Oregon's climate, and what does perimeter slab insulation for a large unheated shop cost?

## Tools, Equipment, and the Owner-Builder Shop

### Earthwork and Excavation Equipment

1. For this specific site's actual soil, slope, and access, does a compact track loader (skid steer class) or a mini excavator do more of the total earthwork scope (driveway cut, footing excavation, utility trenches, septic and drain-field work, block-yard pad prep), and does that argue for owning one and renting the other, or renting both?

Lands in: Tools, Equipment, and the Owner-Builder Shop. [COST] [TOOLS]
Leads: national averages found (1-2 ton $150-250/day, 6-8 ton $400-500/day, weekly ~$289/day effective) need replacement with an actual Oregon quote.

5. What does a realistic used-equipment purchase actually cost and risk, for the specific machine classes this project would buy outright (a compactor, a small mixer, possibly a compact loader), sourced through an online auction versus a local used-equipment dealer versus a private-party sale, including inspection, transport, and the no-warranty risk of an auction buy?

## The Owner-Builder's Body and Mind

### Back, Lifting, and General Body Mechanics

1. The realistic solo lift ceiling for this specific build's materials. Using the NIOSH Revised Lifting Equation, what recommended weight limit applies to lifting an 8x8x16 CMU (36-42 lb) from a pallet at ground level to a scaffold at chest height, repeated all day, versus lifting an 80-lb bag of mix or a 94-lb bag of cement from a truck bed? State the practical solo-lift ceiling that follows, distinct from the general "35-50 lb" figure already flagged elsewhere.
Lands in: The Owner-Builder's Body and Mind. [CODE] [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]
Leads: NIOSH Applications Manual for the Revised Lifting Equation; ideal-condition RWL ceiling is 51 lb, falling sharply with horizontal distance, vertical travel, and frequency.

2. Lifting technique: what current ergonomics research recommends, beyond "lift with your knees." Does the modern research (NIOSH, CCOHS, occupational-therapy literature) support a single correct lifting posture, or does it treat the lift as a multi-factor problem? What does that mean concretely for setting a block, lifting a mixer, or lifting a sheet of plywood alone?
Lands in: The Owner-Builder's Body and Mind. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

3. Minimizing transfer count as a body-mechanics decision, not just a logistics one. For this specific site, what material-staging layout (yard, block-curing area, pallet storage, mixing station, scaffold) minimizes the number of times each unit is picked up between manufacture and final placement in the wall?
Lands in: The Owner-Builder's Body and Mind / Owner-Made Blocks and Alternative Concretes. [METHOD] [CREATIVE-IDEA]

4. Two-person lift thresholds: which specific tasks in this build cross the line from "hard alone" to "unsafe alone" regardless of technique? Ridge beams, precast lintels, large window/door units, a full mortar tub, a genset, a mixer drum full of mix. Build a task-by-task list with approximate weights.
Lands in: The Owner-Builder's Body and Mind. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

5. Core and posterior-chain conditioning specific to masonry and concrete work. What pre-build conditioning program is supported by real strength-and-conditioning literature for the specific movement patterns this build requires: loaded carries, repeated hip-hinge lifting, static kneeling, overhead reaching?
Lands in: The Owner-Builder's Body and Mind. [METHOD] [REAL-WORLD-EXPERIENCE]

6. Daily and pre-shift stretching: is the "stretch and flex" evidence real, and what does a genuinely useful solo routine look like? What is the actual evidence behind the cited 30-percent lost-workday reduction, and what would a 5-10 minute solo pre-shift routine cover for this trade specifically?
Lands in: The Owner-Builder's Body and Mind. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

### Knees, Shoulders, Hands and Wrists

7. Height-adjustable work platforms for the owner-builder: what is available and buildable at owner-builder scale and cost? Commercial adjustable trestles versus a shop-built adjustable platform: compare cost, adjustment speed, and stability for a solo user working both the inner CMU wythe and the outer brick wythe at different heights simultaneously.
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [TOOLS] [COST] [CREATIVE-IDEA]
Leads: commercial adjustable trestles (Butlin, SafeSmart, others, mostly Australian/UK trade brands); US-market equivalents not yet identified.

9. The knee-osteoarthritis dose-response finding and what it implies for a multi-year, thousands-of-hours kneeling exposure. Quantify the cumulative kneeling-hours risk for a solo builder over a multi-year block-and-brick build, and state what practical exposure-reduction measures reduce cumulative hours rather than only cushioning them.
Lands in: The Owner-Builder's Body and Mind. [PROBLEM-SOLUTION] [METHOD]

10. Shoulder-sparing sequencing: does laying order change cumulative overhead-reach exposure? What sequencing and platform-height combination keeps the working course between knee and shoulder height for the largest share of the job?
Lands in: Masonry Craft. [METHOD] [PROBLEM-SOLUTION]

11. Trowel technique and grip-load reduction for a solo mason without a trained tender correcting mortar consistency. 13. Building a Table 1 compliance sheet for every silica-generating task in this specific build. For every task this build will perform, what does OSHA's Table 1 (29 CFR 1926.1153) specify as the required engineering control and respiratory protection? Build the complete task-by-task table.

### Hearing, Eyes, and Vibration
